FROST
Incremental Graph Neural Network Inference
for Geometric Rollouts
Edge-Local Caching with Conservative-by-Construction Staleness,
applied to machine-learned interatomic potentials and learned physics simulators

Thesis. Machine-learned interatomic potentials carry intrinsic force errors of
20 meV/Å to 50 meV/Å against their density-functional-theory reference, yet
every production molecular-dynamics engine evaluates them to floating-point
precision at every timestep. The field computes an approximate function exactly.
Frost reclaims the gap between model error and machine precision as a systems
resource, using an edge-local cache whose invalidation predicate is derived from
the physics of atomic motion rather than from heuristics, and whose staleness is
conservative by construction so that energy drift admits a proved bound rather
than an empirical hope.

A Research Proposal and System Design Document
Target venues: MLSys, USENIX ATC, EuroSys

TARGET HARDWARE
2 × NVIDIA RTX A6000 (48 GB, GA102, SM 8.6) over PCIe Gen4 ×16
Extension path: NVIDIA A100 80 GB, H100 SXM (collaborator allocation)

Version 1.0

•

Document compiled August 21, 2026

F ROST: I NCREMENTAL GNN I NFERENCE FOR G EOMETRIC ROLLOUTS

Abstract
Machine-learned interatomic potentials (MLIPs) built on equivariant message-passing graph neural
networks — NequIP, MACE, Allegro, SevenNet, MatterSim — have displaced density functional
theory (DFT) as the workhorse of atomistic simulation, enabling molecular dynamics (MD) of
104 –106 atoms at near-quantum accuracy. Their cost, however, remains the binding constraint on
accessible timescales: a single nanosecond of dynamics requires 106 timesteps, and each timestep
demands a full forward pass and a full backward pass, because forces are obtained as Fi = −∇ri E.
This proposal begins from an observation that the atomistic simulation community has not
exploited and the machine-learning systems community has not noticed. Contemporary MLIPs
exhibit force errors of 20 meV/Å to 50 meV/Å relative to their DFT reference. Meanwhile, in
MD at 300 K with a 1 fs integration step, a typical atom displaces by roughly 5 × 10−3 Å against a
model cutoff radius of 5 Å to 6 Å — a perturbation of order 0.1%. Production engines nonetheless
recompute the entire graph neural network, to floating-point precision, at every one of those 106
steps. The arithmetic precision of the computation exceeds the physical fidelity of the model by
three orders of magnitude, and the excess is spent uniformly across every atom regardless of whether
its local environment changed at all.
We propose Frost, a molecular dynamics runtime that treats the message-passing state of an
MLIP as a cache with a physics-derived invalidation predicate. Frost makes three contributions.
First, we formalise tolerance-matched computation: the invalidation threshold is tied to the model’s
own validated error bar rather than to machine epsilon, converting a modelling fact into a scheduling
budget. Second, we introduce edge-local caching with attenuated closure. We show analytically
and empirically that caching post-aggregation node features is unworkable — a single moved atom
dirties its entire M × rc receptive field, and in condensed phases the dirty set saturates within one
timestep — whereas caching pre-aggregation edge quantities, which at the first message-passing
layer are pure functions of a single edge’s geometry, propagates not at all. For deeper layers we
replace binary dirtiness with a propagated error bound that attenuates through aggregation, exploiting the same over-smoothing phenomenon that motivates message-passing pruning in atomistic
foundation models. Third, we make staleness conservative by construction: within a refresh interval
Frost differentiates through the cached graph, so the forces it produces are the exact gradient of a
e Drift therefore cannot accumulate within an interval and enters
well-defined surrogate energy E.
only at refresh boundaries as a bounded discrete jump, giving a provable total-drift bound — in
contrast to distilled multiple-time-stepping schemes, which knowingly leave the conservative regime
and must recover stability architecturally.
Crucially, none of this is chemical. The closure-saturation theorem refers only to radius
graphs, message-passing depth, and node density, so it holds for any Lagrangian rollout GNN. We
therefore evaluate Frost across three model families and two research communities: equivariant
interatomic potentials (MACE, NequIP, Allegro, SevenNet), particle-based learned simulators (GNS
on WaterDrop, Sand, Goop), and mesh-based learned simulators (MeshGraphNets on FlagSimple,
DeformingPlate, SphereDynamic), with an Eulerian CFD case (CylinderFlow) as a negative control
where the mechanism should and must fail. In the learned-simulator setting the tolerance argument
is in one respect stronger than in chemistry: these models are trained with input-noise augmentation
precisely because rollout inputs are corrupted, so the training noise scale is a published hyperparameter naming a perturbation magnitude the model was optimised to absorb. The correctness
machinery is correspondingly weaker there — there is no Hamiltonian, hence no conservation
oracle and no drift theorem — and we account for this asymmetry explicitly rather than eliding it.
We present a complete system design: cache layout and compaction, a fused invalidationand-gather kernel, Triton implementations of the tensor-product path, CUDA-graph capture under
dynamic shapes, a two-GPU split placing the cache and the hot subgraph on separate devices across
PCIe, and integration surfaces for ASE, TorchSim, and LAMMPS. We specify an evaluation across
five model families (MACE, NequIP, Allegro, SevenNet, ORB), eleven datasets and benchmark systems (MPtrj, OMat24, MatPES, Matbench Discovery, SPICE2, MD17/rMD17, MD22, 3BPA, liquid
water, silica glass, Li3 PO4 ), and eight baselines including cuEquivariance, FlashTP, BoostMD, and
distilled multiple time stepping. Twenty-nine experiments are specified in advance with quantitative
falsification criteria, so that the proposal can be refuted cheaply: the decisive measurement — the
joint distribution of edge-geometry perturbation and induced force error — requires two weeks, one
GPU, and no new kernels.

F ROST: I NCREMENTAL GNN I NFERENCE FOR G EOMETRIC ROLLOUTS

ii

Keywords: molecular dynamics, machine-learned interatomic potentials, equivariant graph neural
networks, incremental computation, approximate computing, inference systems, energy conservation,
GPU runtime design.

Figure 1: The observation Frost is built on. Machine-learned interatomic potentials are accurate to
tens of meV/Å against their DFT reference, and every production engine evaluates them to floatingpoint precision — 106 times per nanosecond, uniformly across every atom, whether or not its local
environment changed. The gap between what the model is worth and what the arithmetic delivers spans
four to five orders of magnitude, and it is currently spent on nothing. Frost places its tolerance an order
of magnitude below the model’s own validated error, so the approximation sits beneath a noise floor
that already exists. Scales from Table 2.

1

Introduction

1.1

The observation

Two facts about machine-learned interatomic potentials are individually well known and have never, to
our knowledge, been placed side by side.
The first is a fact about accuracy. Contemporary MLIPs are approximate. Benchmarks across the
major architectures place force root-mean-square errors, relative to the density-functional-theory data
on which the models were trained, in the range of roughly 20 meV/Å to 60 meV/Å for well-converged
models on in-distribution systems [42, 3, 4]. Practitioners select models on the accuracy–cost Pareto
front and accept this error as the price of replacing DFT. It is not a small quantity: it is comparable
to the force scale that distinguishes competing polymorphs, and it is the reason the field maintains an
entire subliterature on uncertainty quantification, fine-tuning, and active learning [59, 51, 39].
The second is a fact about dynamics. In molecular dynamics at ambient conditions with the standard
1 fs integration step, atoms barely move. A thermal velocity at 300 K is of order 500 m/s, which is
5 × 10−3 Å fs−1 . Against a model cutoff radius rc of 5 Å to 6 Å and typical bond lengths of 1.5 Å to
2.5 Å, the per-step geometric perturbation is of order 0.1%. The interatomic potential is a smooth
function of these coordinates by construction — smoothness is enforced by the radial cutoff envelope
and is a prerequisite for stable integration.
The gap
The computation is performed in IEEE single or double precision, delivering roughly 10−7 or
10−16 relative accuracy. The model it evaluates is accurate to roughly 10−2 in the relevant physical
units. We are computing an approximate function exactly, and we are doing so 106 times per
nanosecond, uniformly across every atom, regardless of whether that atom’s local environment
changed measurably since the last evaluation.
Between these two facts lies a resource. The distance between the model’s own error bar and the
precision of the arithmetic used to evaluate it is, from a systems perspective, an unclaimed budget. Frost
is a proposal to claim it.

F ROST: I NCREMENTAL GNN I NFERENCE FOR G EOMETRIC ROLLOUTS

1.2

2

Why molecular dynamics is the right setting

Approximate computing has a long history, and it usually founders on the same rock: there is no
principled way to decide how much error is acceptable, so the accuracy dial is set by taste and the
resulting speedups are contested. Molecular dynamics with MLIPs is unusually favourable on exactly
this point, for four reasons.
The tolerance is not a hyperparameter; it is published. Every MLIP ships with a validation force
RMSE. Setting an approximation tolerance ε an order of magnitude below that number is not a taste
judgement — it is a claim that the approximation is invisible beneath the model’s own noise floor, and
it is directly checkable.
Correctness has a ground truth that costs nothing to measure. MD in the microcanonical (NVE)
ensemble conserves total energy exactly in the continuum limit. Energy drift is therefore a complete,
sensitive, cheap, and universally accepted correctness oracle. Unlike image quality in diffusion caching
— where evaluation reduces to perceptual metrics and human judgement — a stale-graph MD run either
conserves energy to within the integrator’s own error or it does not, and the answer is a number.
The redundancy is structural, not incidental. Trajectories are continuous by physical law. Consecutive configurations are not merely similar in the way consecutive diffusion timesteps happen to
be similar; they are related by a smooth flow with a bounded velocity distribution. The redundancy
therefore does not disappear at scale — it increases with system size, since in a larger system the
fraction of atoms in quiescent bulk environments grows relative to the fraction at surfaces, defects, and
reaction sites.
The workload is enormous and cost-bound. Nanosecond-scale MD of 104 –106 atoms is the central
use case for MLIPs, and it is precisely the regime the community identifies as out of reach for atomistic
foundation models. Zhang et al. state the constraint directly: foundation models “run markedly slower
than conventional MLIPs and cannot keep pace with the millions of timesteps required for nanosecondscale MD” [63]. Any multiplicative saving translates directly into accessible physics.

1.3

Why the obvious version of this idea fails

The natural first design is to cache node features. After each message-passing layer, every atom carries
a feature vector; if an atom has not moved, reuse it. This design does not survive contact with the
receptive field.
In a message-passing architecture with M layers and per-layer cutoff rc , information reaches a
distance reff = M × rc [63]. If atom i moves, its layer-1 features change; those feed the layer-2 features
of every atom within rc of i; and so on. The set of atoms whose final features are affected is the M-hop
closure of the moved set. For MACE with M = 2 and rc = 6 Å, the closure of a single atom in a
condensed phase contains several hundred atoms. If even a small fraction of atoms are marked dirty in
a given step — and at 300 K every atom is moving — the closure saturates. We analyse this formally in
§3.2 and show that under a binary dirtiness predicate the expected dirty fraction after closure exceeds
99% for realistic densities at any tolerance tight enough to be physically defensible.
This is, we believe, the reason the idea has not been pursued, and it is why the two existing lines
of work on temporal redundancy in MLIP-driven MD both avoid the problem rather than solve it.
Multiple-time-stepping approaches in the RESPA family [58, 9, 50] split the force, not the atoms,
evaluating a cheap surrogate on most steps and an accurate model occasionally; every atom is treated
identically. BoostMD [8] reuses node embeddings from a reference MLIP at earlier timesteps as input
to a separately trained, faster surrogate; again, the treatment is uniform across atoms and the stride is

F ROST: I NCREMENTAL GNN I NFERENCE FOR G EOMETRIC ROLLOUTS

3

fixed. Neither exploits the spatial structure of the redundancy, because under node-level caching there is
no exploitable spatial structure to find.

1.4

The Frost design in one page

Frost makes three moves that, together, convert an unworkable idea into a system.
Move 1: cache edges, not nodes. The dominant cost in equivariant MLIPs is the Clebsch–Gordan
tensor product between a radial basis expansion R(|ri j |) and spherical harmonics Yℓm (r̂i j ) — which
is why dedicated libraries (cuEquivariance, FlashTP, OpenEquivariance, e3j) exist for this operation
alone [10, 15, 47, 45]. At the first message-passing layer this quantity is a pure function of one edge’s
geometry: no learned node state enters it. Cached at edge granularity and invalidated on a per-edge
geometric predicate, it propagates nowhere. Layer-1 edge caching has exactly zero closure. This single
change makes the idea tractable and, for strictly local architectures such as Allegro [46] where all layers
are edge-local, makes it complete.
Move 2: propagate error, not flags. For deeper message-passing layers, binary dirtiness is the wrong
abstraction because a perturbation does not propagate at full strength. Aggregation over ∼50 neighbours
dilutes any single contribution; the radial envelope decays smoothly to zero at rc ; and successive
message-passing layers are known to become progressively more redundant — the over-smoothing
phenomenon that Zhang et al. exploit for pruning and quantify via linear CKA between successive
layers [63]. Frost therefore propagates a numerical error bound through the closure, marking a node
dirty only when the accumulated attenuated bound exceeds ε. The effective closure radius collapses far
below M × rc . The imported abstraction here is interval arithmetic and abstract interpretation, mature in
programming languages and never applied to this setting.
Move 3: make staleness conservative by construction. Reusing stale features naively produces
forces that are not the gradient of any single energy function. Such non-conservative forces are known
to destabilise integrators [7, 43], and the most recent distilled MTS work accepts non-conservativity
explicitly and must recover stability through architectural priors [50]. Frost avoids the regime entirely.
Within a refresh interval, cached edge features are treated as constants and the force is obtained by
differentiating through the cached graph. The result is the exact gradient of a well-defined surrogate
e hence exactly conservative with respect to a slightly perturbed Hamiltonian. Drift cannot
energy E,
accumulate within an interval; it enters only at refresh boundaries as a discrete jump bounded by the
tolerance, yielding a provable bound on total drift over a trajectory (§5).

1.5

Contributions

1. Tolerance-matched computation (§3). We formalise the observation that MLIP evaluation is
over-precise relative to model error, quantify the available budget across five model families, and
define an approximation tolerance derived from published validation error rather than chosen by
hand.
2. Edge-local caching with attenuated closure (§4). We prove that node-level caching saturates
under realistic conditions (Theorem 3.2), show that edge-local caching at layer 1 has zero closure
(Proposition 3.4), and give an attenuated error-propagation scheme for deeper layers with a proved
bound (Theorem 5.2) whose contraction condition we state as conditional rather than guaranteed,
because worst-case GNN Lipschitz bounds grow exponentially in depth and point the other way
(§7).
3. A C1 error analysis, and the design it forces (§6). Molecular dynamics consumes gradients, not
energies, and bounding a function does not bound its gradient. We show that zeroth-order edge

F ROST: I NCREMENTAL GNN I NFERENCE FOR G EOMETRIC ROLLOUTS

4

caching annihilates the force contribution of every cached edge with an error of Θ(∥J∥) that does
not vanish as the tolerance does (Theorem 6.1). First-order caching repairs this exactly, with force
error linear in displacement (Theorem 6.2), and the invalidation predicate is consequently governed
by the curvature of the edge feature map rather than its slope (Corollary 6.3).
4. Conservative-by-construction staleness (§4.5). We show that differentiating through the cached
graph yields exactly conservative forces with respect to a surrogate Hamiltonian, and derive a total
energy-drift bound linear in the number of refreshes and in ε (Theorem 5.5), with two mechanisms —
envelope switching and refresh-boundary velocity rescaling — that drive the boundary term to zero.
5. A complete system (§8–§10). Cache layout, compaction, fused invalidate-and-gather kernels,
Triton tensor-product variants for the sparse-update path, CUDA-graph capture under dynamic
shapes, a two-GPU cache/compute split over PCIe, and integration surfaces for ASE, TorchSim, and
LAMMPS.
6. Optimality and control (§12). We show the invalidation predicate is the KKT-optimal allocation
policy under a separable error model rather than a heuristic (Theorem 12.1); we close the loop
on accuracy rather than on compute budget, using measured signals — the canary residual and
a physical invariant — that distinguish it from budget-controlled caching [26]; and we state the
performance ceiling explicitly, so every reported speedup is read against the headroom actually
available.
7. Cross-domain generality (§13). The closure-saturation theorem contains no physics, so the mechanism governs any Lagrangian rollout GNN. We evaluate on GNS and MeshGraphNets alongside
interatomic potentials, with an Eulerian CFD case as a negative control where the mechanism must
fail — and we account honestly for the four properties (conservativity, drift bound, free oracle,
reweighting) that do not transfer.
8. A pre-registered evaluation (§11). Twenty-nine experiments across three model families, two
research communities, seventeen systems, and thirteen baselines, each with a falsification criterion
fixed in advance.

1.6

Non-goals

We state these plainly, because a proposal that does not bound its claims invites the wrong review.
• Frost is not a new MLIP architecture. We train no models. Frost consumes pretrained checkpoints
unmodified.
• Frost is not a replacement for kernel-level acceleration. cuEquivariance, FlashTP, and OpenEquivariance optimise the tensor product; Frost decides which tensor products to evaluate. The two compose
multiplicatively, and our evaluation is built on top of them rather than against them.
• Frost does not target reactive or high-temperature regimes without qualification. Bond breaking,
melting, and shock all increase the dirty fraction. We measure the degradation rather than hiding it
(§11.7.9).
• Frost does not target Monte Carlo or global optimisation where consecutive configurations are
uncorrelated by design. Structure relaxation is in scope; random structure search is not.
• Frost makes no claim to improve accuracy. The best possible outcome is that it is indistinguishable
from exact evaluation at lower cost.

F ROST: I NCREMENTAL GNN I NFERENCE FOR G EOMETRIC ROLLOUTS

1.7

5

Document roadmap

§2 gives the necessary background on equivariant MLIP architectures, neighbour-list machinery, and
integrator stability, at a level of detail sufficient for a systems reader without a chemistry background.
§3 presents the motivating analysis, including the formal treatment of closure saturation. §4 presents
the Frost design and §5 its theoretical guarantees. §8 and §10 give the system architecture and low-level
implementation. §11 specifies the evaluation. §17 positions Frost against prior work in atomistic
simulation, diffusion caching, GNN systems, and incremental computation. §18 enumerates threats to
validity, §27 gives a twelve-week schedule, and §29 concludes. Appendices provide notation, kernel
pseudocode, dataset provenance, and a reproducibility checklist.

2

Background

This section establishes the vocabulary and the quantitative facts that the rest of the document relies
on. A systems reader may treat §2.1–§2.2 as the specification of the function being evaluated, and
§2.4–§2.5 as the specification of the loop that evaluates it and the accuracy contract it must honour.

2.1

Potential energy surfaces, forces, and the backward pass

An atomistic configuration is a set of N atoms with positions R = {r1 , . . . , rN }, ri ∈ R3 , chemical
species {Zi }, and (for periodic systems) a lattice h ∈ R3×3 . An interatomic potential is a function
Etot : (R, {Zi }, h) 7−→ E ∈ R

(1)

approximating the Born–Oppenheimer ground-state energy. Forces and the virial stress follow by
differentiation:
∂ Etot
1 ∂ Etot

(2)

V ∂ ε ε=0
Equation (2) is the single most consequential fact for systems design in this domain, and it distinguishes MLIP inference from essentially every other production inference workload. Every evaluation
requires a backward pass. There is no inference-only mode: torch.no_grad() is unusable, activations must be retained through the forward pass, and the memory footprint per configuration is that
of a training step rather than an inference step. This is why practitioners report enabling activation
checkpointing merely to fit systems above 103 atoms on an 80 GB device [49], a technique otherwise
associated exclusively with training.
Two further consequences matter for Frost:
• Conservativity. Because F is a gradient, the force field is curl-free and the dynamics conserve
Etot + K exactly in the continuum limit, where K is the kinetic energy. Any scheme that produces
forces which are not the gradient of a scalar breaks this and injects or removes energy. We return to
this in §2.4.1.
• Sensitivity. Forces are first derivatives, so an approximation with a small effect on E may have a
larger relative effect on F. Any error analysis must be conducted on forces, not energies. Frost’s
tolerance is therefore always stated in force units.

2.2

Equivariant message-passing architectures

Modern MLIPs decompose the total energy into atomic contributions, Etot = ∑i Ei , where Ei depends on
the local environment of atom i within a cutoff rc — the “nearsightedness” assumption originating with
Behler and Parrinello [5]. The dominant family constructs Ei using E(3)-equivariant message passing
on the graph G = (V, E ) with V the atoms and E = {(i, j) : |ri j | < rc } the neighbour pairs.

Here ⊗CG denotes the Clebsch–Gordan tensor product, which couples irreducible representations of
SO(3) so that m transforms correctly under rotation. Equivariance is what buys the data efficiency these
models are known for [4], and the tensor product is what makes them expensive.
2.2.2

Architectures of interest

Table 1 summarises the models we target. The critical structural distinction is between strictly local
architectures, where each atomic energy is a function only of geometric quantities within one cutoff,
and message-passing architectures, where learned state propagates. Allegro was designed for parallel
efficiency precisely by removing node-state propagation [46]; that same design choice makes it perfectly
cacheable, which is why Frost begins there.
2.2.3

Where the FLOPs are

The tensor product dominates. This is not our claim but the community’s, evidenced by the existence of
four independent libraries devoted to accelerating that single operation: NVIDIA’s cuEquivariance [10],
FlashTP [15], OpenEquivariance [47], and the e3j backend introduced with mlip v2, which reports

F ROST: I NCREMENTAL GNN I NFERENCE FOR G EOMETRIC ROLLOUTS

7

up to 3× end-to-end speedup from dedicated CUDA and Pallas kernels for equivariant operations [45].
Benchmarks of MLIP inference consistently report tensor-product-related work as the largest single
contributor [6, 42].
Frost is complementary to all four. Those libraries make each tensor product cheaper; Frost reduces
how many are evaluated. The composition is multiplicative, and §11.7.5 measures it directly.

2.3

Neighbour lists, the skin, and the existing cache

MD engines do not rebuild the neighbour list every step. The standard Verlet scheme builds the list at
an inflated radius rc + s, where s is the skin, and rebuilds only when some atom has moved more than
s/2 since the last build. The cost trade-off is classical: a larger skin means fewer rebuilds but more
candidate pairs filtered per step.
An asymmetry nobody has questioned
Molecular dynamics already caches the graph structure, with an invalidation predicate derived
from atomic displacement. It caches nothing about the features computed on that graph. Frost’s
thesis is that the second cache is at least as valuable as the first, and that the invalidation machinery
for it is a direct generalisation of the machinery MD engines have used for fifty years.
The neighbour-list layer is itself an active systems target. NVIDIA’s ALCHEMI Toolkit moved
neighbour-list construction, structural relaxation, and integration into the PyTorch ecosystem to eliminate host–device transfer overhead, reporting roughly 1.7× acceleration for large systems and 33× for
batched small systems when coupled with TorchSim [2]. TorchSim’s own neighbour-list module was
refactored to support batched calculation across multiple backends [56]. Frost builds on this layer rather
than replacing it.

2.4

Velocity-Verlet is symplectic and time-reversible provided the force is the gradient of a time-independent
potential. Under that condition it exhibits no secular energy drift: the total energy oscillates about a
constant with bounded amplitude O(∆t 2 ).
2.4.1

Energy drift as a correctness oracle

In the NVE ensemble the drift rate

values above roughly 1 meV atom−1 ps−1 indicate a broken simulation. Frost adopts D as its primary
correctness metric because it is sensitive (it integrates error over the whole trajectory rather than
sampling it), cheap (it is a byproduct of the run), and uncontested (no reviewer will argue about whether
energy should be conserved).

2.4.2

Why non-conservative forces are dangerous

The hazard is old and documented. Biesiadecki and Skeel catalogued instabilities in multiple time
stepping [7]; Ma, Izaguirre and Skeel showed that Verlet-I/r-RESPA is limited by nonlinear resonance
instabilities [43]. The contemporary instance is DMTS-NC, which couples an accurate conservative
potential to a distilled representation producing explicitly non-conservative forces, and must impose
architectural priors — equivariance, cancellation of atomic force components — to “significantly limit
abnormal discrepancies” and keep simulations robust [50].
Frost’s third design move (§4.5) is a direct response: rather than managing non-conservativity, we
never enter that regime.

2.5

The error scales that define the budget

Table 2 collects the quantities that set Frost’s tolerance. The essential point is the four-to-five order-ofmagnitude gap between the bottom row and the top row.
Two remarks sharpen the argument.
Remark 2.1 (The precision is already approximate). Most production MLIP inference runs in
FP32 or mixed precision. Benoit’s systematic study of reduced-precision MACE evaluated
FP64/FP32/BF16/FP16 with FP32 accumulation and found regimes where physical fidelity is preserved [6], and 7net-Omni and ORB are benchmarked at float32-high and tf32 settings [49]. The
community has therefore already accepted that evaluation need not be exact. Frost differs in that it
spends the budget selectively in space and time rather than uniformly in mantissa bits — and, unlike
low-precision arithmetic, it spends it where the physics says nothing is happening.
Remark 2.2 (Uniform vs. targeted approximation). Reduced precision degrades every operation equally,
including those on atoms undergoing a reaction. Frost degrades only operations whose inputs have not
measurably changed, and by a bounded amount. The two are composable and their error contributions
are, to first order, independent.

2.6

Batched and parallel MLIP execution

Frost sits in a stack that has developed rapidly. TorchSim rewrote atomistic simulation primitives in
PyTorch and introduced batching across systems of different sizes via scatter operations, with automatic
memory management (“AutoBatching”) in binning, in-flight, and hot-swapping variants [56]. NVIDIA’s
ALCHEMI Batched MD NIM productised dynamic batching of concurrent MD requests based on

F ROST: I NCREMENTAL GNN I NFERENCE FOR G EOMETRIC ROLLOUTS

9

available GPU memory [1]. DistMLIP introduced graph-level partitioning that transfers node and
edge information between GPUs at each layer of the forward pass while maintaining the intermediates
required for backpropagation [13]. Xia and Jiang gave a parallel MPNN algorithm minimising inter-node
communication that scales linearly with layer count and reaches 108 atoms [61].
Every one of these systems reduces the cost of executing a forward–backward pass, or of executing
many in parallel. None reduces the number of message computations that must be performed. Frost is
orthogonal to all of them and composes with each.

3

Motivation and Preliminary Analysis

This section makes the case quantitatively. §3.1 characterises the temporal redundancy available. §3.2
proves that the obvious way to exploit it fails. §3.3 shows why the edge-local reformulation succeeds.
§3.4 motivates attenuated closure from published measurements of over-smoothing. Every claim in
this section is either derived, cited, or listed in §11 as an experiment with a falsification criterion — we
mark predictions as such explicitly rather than asserting them.

3.1
3.1.1

How much redundancy is there?
A back-of-envelope estimate

Consider a monatomic solid at temperature
pT with atomic mass m. The root-mean-square thermal
speed per Cartesian component is vrms = kB T /m. For a mid-mass element (m ≈ 50 u) at 300 K,
vrms ≈ 250 m s−1 = 2.5 × 10−3 Å fs−1 . Over one 1 fs step the displacement is ∼ 2.5 × 10−3 Å; over n
steps, ballistic motion would give n × 2.5 × 10−3 Å, but in a bound environment the displacement is
oscillatory with amplitude set by the vibrational amplitude, typically 0.05 Å to 0.15 Å at 300 K for a
stiff solid.
The quantity that matters is not absolute displacement but the change in edge geometry: ∆ri j and
∆r̂i j . Because neighbouring atoms in a condensed phase move in a correlated fashion (acoustic modes
displace neighbours together), ∆ri j is systematically smaller than ∆|ri |. This is the first reason to
prefer an edge-centric formulation, and it is a genuine physical advantage rather than a bookkeeping
convenience:

∆ri j (t,t0 ) = |ri j (t)| − |ri j (t0 )| ≪ |ri (t) − ri (t0 )| for correlated (long-wavelength) modes.

3.1.2

(9)

The structural argument for scaling

Temporal-redundancy schemes usually degrade at scale. Frost should improve. In a system of N atoms
containing a reaction site, a defect, or an interface, the number of atoms in a “hot” environment grows
sub-linearly in N (as N 2/3 for a surface, O(1) for a point defect) while the number in quiescent bulk
grows as N. The cached fraction therefore increases with system size — the opposite of the usual scaling
story, and directly relevant given that the community’s stated blocker is 104 –106 -atom nanosecond
MD [63].

3.2

Why node-level caching fails: closure saturation

We now prove the negative result that motivates the edge-local design. This analysis is a contribution in
its own right: it explains why the natural idea has not been pursued, and it tells any future worker where
not to look.

Proof. Node j is clean at layer M iff no atom within graph distance M of j is in D0 . Under the

number of such atoms is nM in expectation. Independence of the marks gives the product form.
Corollary 3.3 (The design is unworkable at node granularity). For MACE-class parameters (M = 2,
rc = 6 Å) and a typical condensed-phase density ρ ≈ 0.06 Å−3 (i.e. ∼60 atoms per 1000 Å3 ), we have

Even a 1% raw dirty fraction saturates the closure. At 300 K with any physically defensible δ , essentially

F ROST: I NCREMENTAL GNN I NFERENCE FOR G EOMETRIC ROLLOUTS

11

The negative result
Node-level caching in message-passing MLIPs is not merely inefficient — it is structurally
impossible in condensed phases, because the M-hop closure of any non-trivial dirty set is the
whole system. Any workable design must either (i) operate at a granularity that does not propagate,
or (ii) replace binary dirtiness with something that attenuates. Frost does both.

3.3

The edge-local reformulation

Proposition 3.4 (Zero closure at the first layer). Let gi j = R(1) (ri j ) ⊗CG Yℓm (r̂i j ) denote the geometric
tensor-product factor of the layer-1 message in Equation (3). Then gi j is a function of ri j alone. Its
validity is therefore determined entirely by the invalidation predicate on edge (i, j), and staleness of gi j
propagates to no other edge.
Proof. Immediate from the definition: R(1) and Yℓm take only ri j and r̂i j as arguments. At layer 1 the
(1)
node state h j is a function of the species embedding Z j only, which is constant under the dynamics.
Hence no learned, position-dependent state enters gi j .
This is the pivot of the whole design. It converts an M-hop closure problem into a per-edge decision,
at the cost of caching |E | rather than N objects — typically a factor of 50–100 more entries, but entries
that are independent.
Corollary 3.5 (Allegro is fully cacheable). For strictly local architectures in which no learned node
state is propagated between atoms — Allegro being the canonical instance [46] — every layer satisfies
the hypothesis of Proposition 3.4 with respect to the edge whose geometry it consumes. Closure is
identically zero at all depths.
This corollary determines our experimental strategy. We evaluate Allegro first, where the result is
clean and the mechanism is unencumbered by closure. Only then do we extend to MACE and NequIP,
where layer 1 is exact and deeper layers require §3.4. The project therefore has a defensible result
before its riskiest component is attempted.
3.3.1

What fraction of the model is edge-local?

Table 1 gives our estimates. For MACE with M = 2, layer 1 accounts for roughly 45% of tensor-product
FLOPs; combined with the observation that MACE’s design intent is that “two message-passing layers
suffice” because of high body order [3], the cacheable fraction is substantial even before attenuated
closure is applied to layer 2. Experiment E2 (§11.7.2) measures this exactly by instrumenting the
tensor-product calls rather than estimating.

3.4

Attenuation: why binary dirtiness is the wrong abstraction for deep layers

Theorem 3.2 assumes a perturbation propagates at full strength. Physically it does not, for three
compounding reasons.
(i) Aggregation dilutes. Equation (4) sums over |N (i)| ≈ 30–80 neighbours. A perturbation η on
one incoming message contributes η to a sum of ∼50 terms of comparable magnitude; the relative
√
perturbation of ai is therefore O(η/ n) for uncorrelated perturbations, or O(η/n) in relative terms for
a single dirty edge.
(ii) The radial envelope decays. Every MLIP multiplies the radial basis by a smooth cutoff function
that vanishes with vanishing derivative at rc . Edges near the cutoff — which are the most numerous,
since the number of neighbours in a shell grows as r2 — contribute least, and their perturbations are
attenuated by the envelope.

F ROST: I NCREMENTAL GNN I NFERENCE FOR G EOMETRIC ROLLOUTS

12

(iii) Successive layers become progressively redundant. This is the over-smoothing phenomenon,
and it has been measured in exactly the models we target. Zhang et al. motivate message-passing
pruning of atomistic foundation models by noting that “as message passing grows deeper, node features
tend to homogenize and the incremental” contribution diminishes, and they quantify it with linear CKA
between successive message-passing layers, where “smaller values indicate weaker updates and greater
feature stability” [63]. Their finding is that removing low-contribution layers preserves accuracy. The
same measurement that justifies deleting a layer justifies tolerating stale inputs to it.
Definition 3.6 (Attenuation factor). Define the layer-t attenuation factor
(t+1)

αt = sup
i

∂ hi

(t)

∂ mi j

·

1
,
|N (i)|

(12)

an empirically measurable quantity bounding how much a single perturbed incoming message can
change the updated node state.
We predict αt ≪ 1 and decreasing in t; E3 (§11.7.3) measures it directly and E4 tests whether the
resulting bound is tight enough to be useful. If αt turns out to be O(1), attenuated closure fails and
Frost is restricted to layer-1 and strictly local architectures — a smaller but still complete result, which
is precisely why the evaluation is ordered as it is.

3.5

Design implications

The analysis yields four requirements that the design in §4 must satisfy.
1. R1 — Edge granularity. Cache and invalidate at edge level, not node level (Theorem 3.2, Proposition 3.4).
2. R2 — Quantitative, not binary, staleness. Deep layers require a propagated error bound with
measured attenuation (§3.4).
3. R3 — Tolerance derived from model error. The single user-facing knob must be expressed in
meV/Å and defaulted from the model’s published validation RMSE (§2.5).
4. R4 — Conservativity preserved. The scheme must not produce non-gradient forces, given the documented instabilities [7, 43] and the difficulty DMTS-NC reports in the non-conservative regime [50].

4

The Frost Design

4.1

Overview

Frost interposes between the MD integrator and the MLIP forward–backward pass. Each step, it decides
which edge-level geometric quantities must be recomputed, evaluates the model on the resulting sparse
update, and returns forces obtained by differentiating through the cached graph. Figure 2 shows the
dataflow.
The critical structural point, visible in the figure, is that aggregation is never skipped. Frost caches
the expensive per-edge tensor products; it always re-sums them. This costs a scatter-add over all edges
each step — cheap, bandwidth-bound work — and buys exactness of the aggregation step, which in
turn is what allows the conservativity argument of §4.5 to go through cleanly.

4.2

For MACE-MP-0 medium (128 × 0e + 128 × 1o hidden irreps, ℓmax = 3), the per-edge cached

the backward pass already requires. §10.4 treats the budget precisely and describes FP16 and BF16
cache variants for large systems.
4.2.2

Per-edge reference times
(e)

A design choice worth stating explicitly: each edge carries its own reference time t0 , not a global one.
This is what makes the scheme spatially adaptive. Edges in a quiescent bulk region may go hundreds of
steps without refresh while edges at a reaction site refresh every step. A global reference time would
reduce Frost to a fixed-stride scheme — which is to say, to BoostMD or RESPA.

4.3
4.3.1

The invalidation predicate
From force tolerance to geometric threshold

The user specifies a force tolerance ε in meV/Å. Frost must convert this into a per-edge geometric test.
Because the cache is first-order (§19.1, and mandatorily so by Theorem 6.1), the residual force error is
governed by the curvature of the edge feature map rather than its slope (Corollary 6.3). Define the edge
sensitivity
∂ 2 gi j

so that a geometric perturbation ∥∆ri j ∥ induces a force perturbation bounded by Si j ∥∆ri j ∥ (Theorem 6.2).
Remark 4.1 (Why this is the favourable direction). The original design thresholded on ∥∂ g/∂ ri j ∥.
Because MLIP radial envelopes are smooth with vanishing derivative at rc , the curvature H is typically
smaller than J/rc , so the admissible displacement at a given force tolerance is larger than the slope-based
estimate of Appendix Q suggested. The correction of §6 costs memory and buys reach.
The invalidation predicate is then
(e)

edge (i, j) is dirty ⇐⇒ Si j ri j (t) − ri j (t0 ) > εedge

(14)

√
where εedge = ε/ n̄ apportions the budget across the n̄ edges incident to an atom under an independenterror assumption (§4.6 treats the correlated case).
4.3.2

Making Si j cheap

Computing Si j exactly would defeat the purpose. Frost uses a three-tier scheme:
1. Offline calibration. Before the run, sample ∼ 103 configurations from the target system, compute
Si j exactly by autodiff, and fit a smooth surrogate Ŝ(ri j , Zi , Z j ) — a function of scalar distance and
species pair only. This is a one-time cost of seconds.
2. Online lookup. At runtime, Ŝ is a small table indexed by species pair and binned distance. The
predicate reduces to a comparison against a per-species-pair, per-distance-bin threshold, evaluated in
the same kernel that computes ∆ri j .
3. Conservative fallback. Ŝ is fitted as an upper envelope, not a mean, so the predicate over-invalidates
rather than under-invalidates. Safety is preserved by construction; only efficiency depends on the fit
quality.
The key simplification: because both the radial basis and the spherical harmonics are smooth and
bounded with bounded derivative on [0, rc ], Si j is dominated by the envelope derivative and is largest at
short range — precisely where atoms are most tightly bound and move least. The sensitivity structure
and the motion structure are therefore anti-correlated, which is favourable and is measured in E1.

4.4

Attenuated closure for deep layers

For architectures with M > 1, staleness in a layer-1 edge quantity perturbs the layer-1 node aggregate,
which perturbs layer-2 messages. Frost propagates a bound rather than a flag.
Algorithm 1 runs in O(|E |) per layer with the same access pattern as aggregation — it can in fact
be fused into the aggregation kernel, since both are scatter-adds over the same edge list. The cost is
therefore a small constant factor on an operation that is already bandwidth-bound and already being
performed.
Remark 4.2 (Why this is not merely a heuristic). Algorithm 1 computes an upper bound, not an
estimate, provided αt is measured as a supremum over a calibration set and the envelope weights are
exact (they are — they are cached alongside g). A node marked clean is therefore guaranteed to have
accumulated error below εt . This distinguishes Frost from diffusion-caching heuristics, which use error
indicators without bounds [62, 14, 52].

4.5

Conservative-by-construction staleness

This is the design move that converts an empirical drift plot into a correctness argument.

4.5.1

The construction

Proposition 4.3 (Exact conservativity within an interval). Ee as defined in Equation (15) is a welldefined, single-valued, smooth function of R on the interval [t0 ,t0 + τ). Hence F = −∇Ee is curl-free,
and velocity-Verlet applied to it is symplectic and exhibits no secular drift of Ee + K.
Proof. On the interval, H , C , and the constants ḡ are fixed. Ee is then a composition of smooth
functions of R (the hot tensor-product path) with fixed constants, hence smooth in R. A gradient field
of a smooth scalar is curl-free; the symplecticity of velocity-Verlet for a time-independent conservative
force follows by the standard argument.
The contrast with distilled multiple time stepping
DMTS-NC couples an accurate conservative potential to a distilled representation “optimized for
the production of nonconservative forces”, and must impose architectural priors — equivariance
under rotation, cancellation of atomic force components — to “significantly limit abnormal
discrepancies” between the two models and keep simulations robust [50]. Frost never leaves the
conservative regime, so no such machinery is required and no such fragility is inherited.

F ROST: I NCREMENTAL GNN I NFERENCE FOR G EOMETRIC ROLLOUTS

4.5.2

16

Where error actually enters

Proposition 4.3 means drift cannot accumulate within an interval. Error enters only at refresh boundaries,
where ḡ is updated and the governing Hamiltonian changes discontinuously from Ee(k) to Ee(k+1) . The
jump is
∆k = Ee(k+1) (R(tk )) − Ee(k) (R(tk )),
(17)
which is bounded by the tolerance because the refresh was triggered precisely when the bound reached
ε. §5 makes this a theorem and gives two mechanisms that drive ∆k to zero.

4.6
4.6.1

Refresh policy and error budgeting
Three refresh triggers

1. Predicate trigger (Eq. 14): the edge’s geometry moved enough. This is the primary, spatially
adaptive mechanism.
2. Age trigger: age[e] exceeds τmax . This bounds worst-case staleness for edges whose geometry
oscillates without ever exceeding threshold, and guards against slow systematic drift below the
predicate’s resolution.
3. Keyframe trigger: every Tkey steps, refresh everything. This resets accumulated bookkeeping error
and provides a clean synchronisation point — the direct analogue of the diffusion-caching practice
where, once a critical error line is crossed, “full inference is required to reset accumulated error” [62].
4.6.2

Staggering

A naive keyframe refreshes every edge on the same step, producing a periodic cost spike that destroys
latency predictability and wrecks CUDA-graph reuse. Frost staggers refreshes: edge e has its age
trigger offset by hash(e) mod τmax , so refresh work is spread uniformly across steps. This is a small
implementation detail with a large effect on tail latency and is evaluated in E8.
4.6.3

Budget allocation under correlation
√
The apportionment εedge = ε/ n̄ assumes independent errors. In a solid, neighbour displacements are
correlated by acoustic modes, so errors may add coherently. Frost supports three modes:
√
• independent: ε/ n̄ — optimistic, fastest.
• worst-case: ε/n̄ — assumes full coherence; provably safe.
• measured: calibrate the effective coherence exponent γ ∈ [0.5, 1] from a short pilot run, use ε/n̄γ .
E6 (§11.7.6) measures γ across systems. We default to measured and report all three.

4.7

The complete step

Algorithm 2 shows the complete step. Note what is unchanged: the integrator, the neighbour-list logic,
the aggregation, the readout, and the autodiff mechanism. Frost adds one fused predicate kernel, one
stream compaction, a sparse dispatch, and a write-back. This is the ratio the proposal aims for — two or
three load-bearing new ideas resting on a bed of standard, well-tested machinery.

Theoretical Analysis
Error propagation bound

Assumption 5.1 (Bounded sensitivity). The readout and update maps are Lipschitz on the compact
region of feature space visited by the trajectory, with constants LU (update) and Lro (readout). The
radial envelope w(r) satisfies 0 ≤ w ≤ 1 with w(rc ) = w′ (rc ) = 0.
Assumption 5.1 is mild: MLIPs are built from smooth activations on bounded inputs, and smoothness
is a design requirement for stable MD, not an idealisation.
Theorem 5.2 (Attenuated error bound). Let each cached edge carry error ηi j ≤ εedge and let αt be the
layer-t attenuation factor. Then the error in the final atomic energy satisfies
M−1

|∆Ei | ≤ Lro · εedge · n̄ w̄

∏


αt n̄ w̄ ,

(18)

t=1

where w̄ = E[wi j ] is the mean envelope weight and n̄ the mean degree. The bound contracts, i.e. deep
layers contribute less than shallow ones, iff αt n̄ w̄ < 1.

F ROST: I NCREMENTAL GNN I NFERENCE FOR G EOMETRIC ROLLOUTS

18

Proof. By induction on t. At t = 1 the aggregate error is at most ∑ j wi j ηi j ≤ n̄w̄ εedge . Applying U (1)
multiplies by at most α1 by definition of the attenuation factor; propagating along n̄ envelope-weighted
edges to the next layer multiplies by n̄w̄. Iterating and applying Lro at the readout gives the stated
product.
Corollary 5.3 (Depth is affordable when attenuation is strong). If αt n̄w̄ ≤ β < 1 for all t, the total
error is bounded by a geometric series and is O(εedge ) independent of M. Frost’s viability on deep
architectures is therefore entirely determined by whether β < 1, which is measured directly in E3.
Remark 5.4 (This is falsifiable, and we intend to falsify it if it is false). If E3 returns β ≥ 1 for
NequIP-class depths, Theorem 5.2 does not contract and Frost’s deep-layer path is unusable. The result
would then be: Frost is complete for strictly local architectures and for the first layer of message-passing
architectures, and we report the negative result for deeper layers explicitly. This is a narrower paper, not
a failed one.

5.2

Energy drift bound

Theorem 5.5 (Total drift). Let a trajectory of length T contain K refresh events, and let each refresh
induce an energy jump |∆k | ≤ δmax , where δmax is bounded by the tolerance through Theorem 5.2. Then
the total energy change satisfies
Ee + K

integrator

If refresh jumps are uncorrelated in sign — the√expected case, since they arise from independent local
geometric events — the first term is instead O( K δmax ) by a random-walk argument.
Proof. By Proposition 4.3, on each interval between refreshes the dynamics are symplectic under a fixed
Hamiltonian, so the energy change within an interval is bounded by the integrator’s O(∆t 2 ) oscillation
and has no secular component. The only other changes are the K discrete jumps at boundaries. Summing
gives the worst-case bound; independence of signs gives the random-walk refinement.
Corollary 5.6 (Drift is controllable by a single knob). Since δmax scales linearly with ε and K decreases
monotonically with ε, the drift bound K(ε) δmax (ε) has a computable minimum. Frost can therefore
expose a target drift rate and solve for ε, rather than exposing ε and hoping.

5.3

Driving the boundary term to zero

Theorem 5.5 bounds drift but does not eliminate it. Two mechanisms do.
Mechanism A: envelope switching. Replace the hard cache swap with a smooth crossfade over ns
steps:

smooth in classical force fields, imported directly.
Mechanism

thermostats operate) and costs one reduction. It perturbs the trajectory but conserves energy identically.

(MACE layer 1 only). E1 measures φ in week one, against exactly these thresholds.

Complexity and memory

Two observations. First, the backward pass shrinks along with the forward pass, because the detached
cached tensors need no stored activations — so Frost reduces the activation memory that forces
practitioners into inference-time activation checkpointing [49]. Second, the cache is O(EF) whereas
retained activations are O(EF) per layer; for M ≥ 2 the cache is smaller than the memory it saves. E10
(§11.7.10) tests whether Frost therefore permits larger systems on the same device, which would be a
second contribution of the memory argument.

6

Force-Error Analysis: Why Zeroth-Order Caching Is Inadmissible

This section corrects an error in the design as first presented and, in doing so, promotes the first-order
cache from an optimisation to a requirement. We present it as a standalone analysis because the
reasoning is instructive and because a reader who does not follow it will misjudge the whole design.

6.1

The error

Sections 4.2–4.5 presented a zeroth-order cache: hold gi j at its reference value ḡi j and bound the
resulting energy error by ε. The first-order cache of §19.1 was then offered as an enhancement that
extends cache lifetime.
That framing is wrong, and the reason is that molecular dynamics does not consume energies. It
consumes forces, and forces are gradients.
Theorem 6.1 (Zeroth-order caching annihilates the force contribution). Let Etot = ∑i Ei (ai ) with ai =
∑ j∈N (i) gi j , and let C ⊆ E be the cached edge set with gi j ≡ ḡi j held constant. Then for any atom k,
Fcached
− Fexact
=
k
k

∂ Ei

∑ ∂ ai ∑
i

j : (i, j)∈C
k∈{i, j}

∂ gi j
,
∂ rk

(24)

whose magnitude is Θ(|Ck | · ∥J∥), where Ck are the cached edges incident to k and J = ∂ g/∂ ri j . This
is independent of the displacement ∥∆ri j ∥ and does not vanish as the tolerance ε → 0.
Proof. ḡi j is a constant with respect to R, so ∂ ḡi j /∂ rk = 0 identically. Differentiating Ee therefore omits
every term arising from a cached edge. The omitted terms are exactly those in the sum above, and each
has magnitude Θ(∥J∥) regardless of how recently the edge was refreshed.

F ROST: I NCREMENTAL GNN I NFERENCE FOR G EOMETRIC ROLLOUTS

21

What this means
A zeroth-order cache does not approximate the force — it deletes the force contribution of every
cached edge. At a dirty fraction of φ = 0.1, ninety per cent of each atom’s force contributions
vanish. The error is not small, is not controlled by ε, and does not shrink as the cache is refreshed
more aggressively. It is a structural defect, not an accuracy–speed trade-off.
The consequence is that our earlier presentation had the design ordering backwards. The firstorder cache is not an enhancement. It is the minimum admissible design.

6.2

Why the earlier energy-only analysis missed it

Theorem 5.2 bounds |∆Ei |. Bounding a function does not bound its gradient: ∥ f ∥∞ small does not imply
∥∇ f ∥∞ small, and here the failure is maximal — ∆E is O(ε) while ∇∆E is O(∥J∥). Any error analysis
for a gradient-consuming workload must be conducted in C1 , not C0 . We had noted in §2.1 that forces
are more sensitive than energies and that the tolerance must therefore be stated in force units; we did
not follow that observation through to the cache design.

6.3

The first-order cache repairs it exactly

With ĝi j (ri j ) = ḡi j + Ji j (ri j − rref
i j ),
∂ ĝi j
= Ji j ,
∂ ri j

(25)

which is the exact Jacobian evaluated at the reference geometry rather than at the current one. The force
contribution is therefore preserved to first order, and the error becomes a genuine perturbation:
Theorem 6.2 (First-order force error). Under Assumption 5.1 with the additional requirement that g is
C2 with ∥∂ 2 g/∂ r2i j ∥ ≤ H, the force error induced by first-order caching of edge (i, j) satisfies
∆F(i j)

where LE = ∥∂ Ei /∂ ai ∥ and LE′ = ∥∂ 2 Ei /∂ a2i ∥. The bound is O(∥∆ri j ∥) and vanishes as ε → 0.
Proof. Two sources of error. (i) The stored Jacobian Ji j is evaluated at rref rather than at ri j ; by the
mean value theorem the discrepancy is bounded by H∥∆ri j ∥, and it enters the force contracted with
∂ E /∂ a. (ii) The stale value ĝ differs from g by the second-order Taylor remainder 12 H∥∆ri j ∥2 , which
perturbs ai and hence ∂ Ei /∂ ai , entering the force through the second derivative of E . Summing gives
the stated bound.
Corollary 6.3 (The predicate must be recalibrated). The invalidation predicate (Equation 14) must
threshold on the force error of Theorem 6.2, which is dominated by the linear term LE H∥∆ri j ∥. Hence
the correct sensitivity surrogate is
Ŝi j ≈ LE ∂ 2 gi j /∂ r2i j ,

(27)

i.e. it is governed by the curvature of the edge feature map, not its slope. The calibration procedure of
Appendix B is amended accordingly: the Hutchinson probe estimates H rather than J.
This is a strictly more favourable situation than the original design implied. Because H enters where
J did, and H is typically smaller than J/rc for the smooth radial envelopes MLIPs use, the geometric
threshold at a given force tolerance is larger than Appendix Q estimated.

6.4

Consequences for the rest of the document

6.5

An unexpected benefit: E1 becomes cheaper and sharper

Theorem 6.1 makes the week-one experiment more decisive. Rather than measuring a dirty fraction and
inferring a speedup, E1 now measures a quantity with a sharp theoretical prediction attached:
• Zeroth-order caching must show force error O(∥J∥), independent of ε. If it does not, our understanding
of the model’s structure is wrong and everything downstream is suspect.
• First-order caching must show force error scaling linearly in ∥∆ri j ∥ with slope LE H.
• The ratio of the two at fixed ∆r is a direct measurement of ∥J∥/H — a property of the architecture
that, to our knowledge, nobody has reported.
The experiment therefore validates a theorem before it gates a project, which is a better use of two
weeks than a speedup estimate.

7

Grounding Attenuation in GNN Stability Theory

Section 3.4 introduced the attenuation factor αt as an empirically measured quantity. It is in fact a local
Lipschitz constant of the layer update map, and there is a substantial literature on Lipschitz analysis
of message-passing networks that we should engage rather than reinvent. Doing so produces one
favourable result and one distinctly unfavourable one, and the unfavourable one is more important.

7.1

The unfavourable result: worst-case bounds do not contract

Stability analyses of graph convolutional networks give explicit perturbation bounds of the form


E ∥Φ̃(x) − Φ(x)∥22 ≤ C(1 − p)∥x∥22 + O((1 − p)2 ),
C = n α CL2 L2Cσ2L F 2L−2 ,
(28)

F ROST: I NCREMENTAL GNN I NFERENCE FOR G EOMETRIC ROLLOUTS

23

where L is depth, F the feature width, Cσ the nonlinearity’s Lipschitz constant, CL the filter’s integral
Lipschitz constant, and α the maximal node degree or 2 [35]. Related work derives comparable bounds
for GCN, GraphSAGE and GAT [36] and for GIN with respect to a tree-based graph metric [33].
The structure of that constant is decisive for us: it grows as Cσ2L F 2L−2 , i.e. exponentially in
depth and polynomially in width. A worst-case Lipschitz analysis therefore predicts perturbation
amplification, not attenuation.
Remark 7.1 (We must not claim contraction from theory). Theorem 5.2’s contraction condition
αt n̄w̄ < 1 is not implied by any global Lipschitz bound in this literature — the global bounds go the
other way. Presenting attenuation as a theoretical guarantee would be wrong, and a reviewer familiar
with GNN stability theory would catch it. We therefore state Theorem 5.2 as conditional on a measured
β , and treat E3 as the experiment that decides whether the condition holds.

7.2

Why the worst-case bounds are nonetheless not binding here

Three reasons the global constants are pessimistic for Frost’s setting, each of which is itself a hypothesis
E3 tests:
(i) Local, not global. Frost needs a local Lipschitz constant on the thin manifold of configurations a
trajectory visits, not a global one over all inputs. The literature is explicit that global constants “have
to hold even for distinct input points and cannot characterize the local behavior of neural networks”,
which motivates dedicated machinery for local constants via Clarke-Jacobian bound propagation and
branch-and-bound [38]. We adopt the local formulation and estimate it by sampling on the trajectory
itself.
(ii) Over-smoothing acts against amplification. The over-smoothing literature establishes that repeated message passing drives node representations toward a low-dimensional subspace, with Dirichlet
energy decaying under a contraction condition on the update map [37]. Over-smoothing is a pathology
for expressivity and a gift for us: the same mechanism that homogenises features also damps perturbations. This is the rigorous version of the CKA argument in §3.4, and it lets us state the connection
precisely.
Proposition 7.2 (Dirichlet-energy contraction implies attenuation). Let the layer update satisfy a global
contraction with rate δ < 1 in the sense used in the over-smoothing literature, so that the Dirichlet
energy of the node-feature field obeys D(h(t+1) ) ≤ δ D(h(t) ). Then a perturbation confined to a√single
edge, which is a high-Dirichlet-energy (maximally non-smooth) signal, is damped by at least δ per
layer in the ℓ2 norm.
Proof sketch. A single-edge perturbation is orthogonal to the constant mode and therefore has Dirichlet
energy bounded below by λ2 ∥η∥2 where λ2 is the spectral gap. Contraction of D by δ combined with
the lower bound gives the claim after one layer; iterating gives δ t/2 .
Proposition 7.2 is the reason we expect β < 1 despite the worst-case bounds. It also predicts
something testable: attenuation should be stronger on graphs with a larger spectral gap, i.e. wellconnected condensed phases rather than sparse molecular systems. E3 measures αt against λ2 to test
this.
(iii) Smooth envelopes are integral-Lipschitz filters. MLIP radial envelopes vanish with vanishing
derivative at rc , which places them in the integral-Lipschitz class for which stability holds uniformly
regardless of eigenvalue magnitude [34]. The design choice made for physical smoothness therefore
also buys the stability property Frost’s analysis needs — an alignment worth naming.

7.3

What E3 must now report

Reporting the gap between the global bound and the measured local constant is, we think, a small
contribution to the GNN stability literature independent of Frost: it is a measurement of how loose
worst-case Lipschitz bounds are on a real, deployed, physically constrained architecture.

8

System Architecture

8.1

Component decomposition

Figure 3 shows the five-layer decomposition. The design constraint we imposed on ourselves is that no
model implementation is modified. Frost wraps a model by intercepting the tensor-product call sites,
which are already isolated behind a backend abstraction in every target implementation (this is how
cuEquivariance and FlashTP are swapped in). This constraint is what makes evaluation across five
model families feasible in a twelve-week project.

8.2
8.2.1

Cache manager
Layout

The cache is a structure-of-arrays keyed by a stable edge identifier. Because the neighbour list is rebuilt
periodically and edges enter and leave, edge identity cannot be a raw index into the current neighbour

25

list. Frost uses a hash of the ordered pair (idi , id j , npbc ), where npbc is the periodic image offset, into an
open-addressed table sized at 1.5× the expected edge count.

Listing 1: Cache layout (device-side view)

8.2.2

Handling neighbour-list rebuilds

When the Verlet skin criterion fires, the edge set changes. Frost handles this without invalidating the
whole cache:
• Edges present before and after: cache entry retained, matched by key. This is the common case — a
skin rebuild typically changes a small percentage of pairs.
• New edges: inserted as dirty, forcing computation on the next step.
• Departing edges: entries freed lazily by tombstoning; compaction runs when the load factor exceeds
0.75.
This is the single most important engineering detail for correctness. A subtle bug here — a stale
entry matched to a new edge with the same key — would corrupt forces silently. §10.6 describes the
differential test that guards it.

8.3

Invalidation engine

The invalidation engine evaluates Equation (14) for every edge. Implemented naively this is a separate
kernel launch reading ri j and r_ref — pure bandwidth, roughly 24 B/edge read, and at 5 × 105 edges
around 12 MB per step, which at A6000 bandwidth (768 GB/s) is ∼ 16 µs. That is not negligible against
a MACE step of a few milliseconds, but it is small; and §10.2 describes the fusion that removes most of
it.
The engine also maintains the sensitivity table Ŝ. Because Ŝ depends only on (Zi , Z j , binned ri j ), it
is a small constant-memory lookup: for a system with nZ species and nb = 64 distance bins, the table is
n2Z × nb floats — for a five-component alloy, 6 kB, fitting entirely in L1.

8.4

Refresh scheduler

The scheduler owns the three triggers of §4.6 and the staggering policy. It also implements admission
control on refresh work: if the dirty fraction in a step exceeds a ceiling φmax (default 0.9), Frost degrades

gracefully to a full recompute, which is both faster (no compaction overhead, dense kernel path, CUDAgraph reuse) and simpler. This means Frost’s worst case is bounded at approximately the baseline cost
plus predicate overhead — an important property for a system claiming to be safe to enable by default.

8.5

Two-GPU organisation

The target hardware is two A6000s over PCIe with no NVLink. This is a constraint for conventional
MLIP parallelism — DistMLIP’s graph partitioning transfers node and edge information at every layer
of the forward pass [13], which is latency-sensitive — but it suits Frost, because Frost’s natural split has
a very different communication profile.
Two configurations are implemented:
Configuration R (replicated). Each GPU owns a spatial half of the system with a halo of width Mrc ,
and holds the cache for its own edges. No cache traffic crosses PCIe; only halo positions and forces do,
which is the standard domain-decomposition pattern and is kilobytes. This is the default and the one we
expect to use for all large-system results.
Configuration S (split). GPU1 holds the full cache; GPU0 computes. Dirty edge geometry goes
GPU0→GPU1, refreshed tensors return. Interesting when the cache exceeds a single device’s memory
— i.e. for the largest systems — and the transfer volume scales with φ , so it becomes cheaper as the
cache gets colder. E11 evaluates both.
Remark 8.1 (The PCIe constraint is not a handicap here). It is worth naming explicitly: on this workload
the absence of NVLink costs Frost very little, because Frost’s inter-device traffic is proportional to the
dirty fraction rather than to the model width. A group with an NVLink-connected pair would measure
approximately the same speedups. This is unusual and we regard it as a reason the work is well matched
to the available hardware rather than a limitation to be apologised for.

§8 gave the component decomposition. This section specifies the runtime in enough detail to be
implemented: the cache entry lifecycle, the per-step control flow and kernel timeline, the refresh
decision logic, the memory hierarchy, and the two multi-GPU dataflows.

Halo edge ownership. In Configuration R an edge crossing the domain boundary is visible to both

10.2
10.2.1

Kernel design
Fused predicate and gather

The naive pipeline is: (1) kernel computes ∆ri j and writes a mask; (2) CUB compaction produces
indices; (3) gather kernel collects dirty edge geometry; (4) tensor-product kernel. Steps 1 and 3 both
read edge data, wasting bandwidth on a bandwidth-bound operation.

F ROST: I NCREMENTAL GNN I NFERENCE FOR G EOMETRIC ROLLOUTS

29

Frost fuses them. A single kernel walks the neighbour list, computes ∆ri j , evaluates Ŝ from
shared-memory-resident tables, and — for edges that fail the test — writes the geometry directly into a
compacted output buffer using an atomic counter, rather than writing a mask to be compacted later.

Listing 2: Fused invalidate-and-gather (abridged)
The warp-aggregated atomic in Listing 2 matters: a per-thread atomicAdd on a single global
counter would serialise catastrophically at low dirty fractions. With ballot aggregation there is one
atomic per warp, and at φ = 0.05 most warps contribute nothing.

Sparse tensor-product dispatch

Once compacted, the hot edges form a dense contiguous array and can be fed to any existing tensorproduct kernel unmodified — cuEquivariance, FlashTP, or OpenEquivariance all accept a batch of edge
geometries. This is a deliberate design choice: Frost does not reimplement the hard kernel. It changes
the batch that kernel receives.
We nonetheless provide a Triton variant for two reasons: (i) to handle very small hot sets, where
library kernels tuned for large batches launch-dominate, and (ii) to fuse the write-back into the cache
with the computation, saving a round trip. The Triton path is selected automatically when φ E falls
below a tuned threshold.
10.2.3

Scatter-add aggregation

Aggregation is unchanged and always exact, running over all E edges. This is the operation whose
cost Frost does not reduce, and it therefore sets the ceiling on achievable speedup — exactly the θ
parameter in Table 5. It is bandwidth-bound and benefits from the standard treatments (sorting edges by
destination for locality, segmented reduction to avoid atomics).

10.3

CUDA graphs under dynamic shapes

MLIP MD steps are short — microseconds to milliseconds — so launch overhead matters, and CUDA
graph capture is standard practice. Frost’s hot-set size φ E varies per step, which naively defeats capture.
Frost uses bucketed capture: hot-set sizes are rounded up to the next power of two, and a graph is
captured per bucket, with the tail padded by replicating a dummy edge whose contribution is masked
out. With buckets from 28 to 221 this is 14 graphs, captured lazily on first use. Padding waste is at most
2× in the worst case and ∼1.33× in expectation, which is far cheaper than losing graph capture entirely.
The technique is borrowed directly from LLM serving, where the same problem arises with variable
batch sizes.

10.4

Memory budget

Table 12 contains a claim worth flagging as a prediction: Frost total is lower than baseline activations.
The reason is that detached cached tensors require no stored activations for the backward pass, so
Frost trades M layers of retained activations for one cache. If the prediction holds, Frost enables larger
systems on the same hardware — a second, independent contribution, and one directly responsive to the
reported need for inference-time activation checkpointing above 1,000 atoms [49]. E10 tests it.

10.5

Numerical precision of the cache

The cache may be stored in lower precision than the computation, because its contents are already
approximate by design. We implement FP32, BF16, and FP16 variants. BF16 is expected to be the
sweet spot: its 8-bit exponent matches FP32’s dynamic range (important for tensor-product outputs

F ROST: I NCREMENTAL GNN I NFERENCE FOR G EOMETRIC ROLLOUTS

31

spanning several orders of magnitude across irrep orders), and its reduced mantissa contributes an
error comparable to εedge — i.e. it is free within the existing budget. This composes with, rather than
duplicates, the reduced-precision work of Benoit [6], and E12 measures the interaction.

10.6

Testing and validation

Silent numerical corruption is the principal engineering risk. Three guards:
1. Differential test at ε = 0. With tolerance zero, every edge is dirty every step and Frost must
reproduce the baseline bitwise (up to reduction-order non-determinism). This exercises the entire
cache, compaction, and dispatch path while removing the approximation, isolating implementation
bugs from approximation error. Run on every commit.
2. Gradient check. Finite-difference validation of F = −∇Ee on the cached graph, confirming that the
detach is placed correctly. A misplaced detach is the most likely way to silently break Proposition 4.3.
3. Long NVE canary. A 100 ps NVE run on a stiff system with drift monitoring, executed nightly.
Drift is the integral of error over the trajectory and catches faults that per-step comparisons miss.

10.7

Integration surfaces

• ASE [41]: Frost presents as a Calculator wrapping any existing MLIP calculator. One-line change
for the user. This is the path for MD17, MD22, 3BPA, and small-molecule work.
• TorchSim [56]: Frost registers as a model wrapper, inheriting batched simulation and autobatching.
This gives access to the high-throughput relaxation workloads and to Matbench Discovery scale
without reimplementing batching.
• LAMMPS [55] via ML-IAP: the production path for large-system MD, and the one required for
credible ns/day comparisons against SevenNet and MACE as deployed.

11
11.1

Evaluation Plan
Research questions

RQ1 How much temporal redundancy exists at edge granularity, across systems, temperatures, and phases,
and how does it depend on the tolerance ε?
RQ2 What fraction of MLIP inference cost is edge-local and therefore cacheable, per architecture?
RQ3 Does attenuated closure contract (β < 1) for real message-passing architectures, and how far below
Mrc does the effective closure radius fall?
RQ4 Does conservative-by-construction staleness deliver the drift bound of Theorem 5.5 in practice, and
do the zero-boundary mechanisms work?
RQ5 What end-to-end speedup does Frost achieve, and how does it compose with cuEquivariance, FlashTP,
and reduced precision?
RQ6 Are physical observables — RDF, VDOS, diffusion coefficients, elastic constants, phase stability —
preserved within statistical error?
RQ7 How does Frost compare with BoostMD and distilled MTS, which attack the same redundancy with
model-level methods?
RQ8 Does Frost reduce peak memory enough to enable larger systems?

11.3.1

Scale

20 systems

Why this dataset set and not another

Three principles governed selection. First, public and reproducible: every system listed is either in
a public dataset or constructible in a few lines of ASE/pymatgen. Second, spanning the predicted
range: Table 3 predicts Frost works best on stiff, correlated, non-diffusive systems and worst on reactive
ones; the selection deliberately includes the cases predicted to fail. Third, comparable to published
numbers: diamond Si and Li3 PO4 appear specifically because published per-atom step times exist for
them [49, 63], so our baselines can be externally checked rather than self-reported.

F ROST: I NCREMENTAL GNN I NFERENCE FOR G EOMETRIC ROLLOUTS

11.4

35

Baselines

The baseline that decides the paper
Fixed-stride caching is the honest bar. “Recompute everything every k steps” is trivial to implement, almost certainly exists in practitioners’ scripts even if not in papers, and captures all of the
temporal redundancy while capturing none of the spatial redundancy. Frost’s entire claim reduces
to: spatial adaptivity beats a stride. Every speedup figure in the paper will show the stride sweep
alongside Frost, and if the curves coincide we will say so.

11.6

Hardware and reproducibility

Primary: 2× RTX A6000 (48 GB, GA102, SM 8.6), PCIe Gen4 ×16, CUDA 12.x, PyTorch 2.x.
Extension: A100 80 GB and H100 SXM through a collaborator allocation, used for cross-generation
validation (E13). All runs use fixed seeds, pinned library versions, and three repetitions with reported
confidence intervals. Because MLIP inference is already non-deterministic across batch composition
in FP32, we report medians with interquartile ranges rather than means, and we state reduction-order
settings explicitly.

11.7
11.7.1

Experiments
E1 — Redundancy characterisation (the go/no-go)

Setup. Allegro and MACE-MP-0 on five systems (Si, Li3 PO4 , liquid water, silica glass, Cu). NVT at
300 K and 1000 K, 10,000 steps. Log per-edge ∆ri j , ∆r̂i j against per-edge reference; sweep ε over four
decades.
Outputs. (i) Dirty fraction φ (ε) per system and temperature. (ii) The joint distribution of edge
perturbation and induced force error — this plot is the paper’s Figure 1. (iii) Spatial autocorrelation of
the dirty mask, quantifying how much structure spatial adaptivity has to exploit.
Falsification. If φ > 0.5 at the largest ε whose induced force error stays below 5 meV/Å, on the
systems predicted to be favourable (silica glass, Si), the mechanism does not deliver and the project
stops. Table 5 sets the exact thresholds. Cost: two weeks, one GPU, no new kernels.

F ROST: I NCREMENTAL GNN I NFERENCE FOR G EOMETRIC ROLLOUTS

11.7.2

36

E2 — Cost decomposition

Instrument every tensor-product call in all five architectures with CUDA events and Nsight Compute.
Report θ , the cacheable share, per model and per layer. Falsification: θ < 0.35 for MACE would cap
speedup below 1.5× even at φ = 0.
11.7.3

E3 — Attenuation measurement

Measure αt per layer by perturbing single cached edges and observing downstream change; compute β =
maxt αt n̄w̄. Cross-check against linear CKA between successive layers, following [63]. Falsification:
β ≥ 1 restricts Frost to layer 1 and strictly local models (reported as a negative result, not hidden).
11.7.4

E4 — Effective closure radius

Measure the actual spatial extent over which a perturbation exceeds ε, versus the nominal Mrc . Success:
effective radius ≤ 0.5 Mrc , which would approximately cube the clean fraction relative to binary closure.
11.7.5

E5 — End-to-end speedup and kernel composition

Frost × {cuEquivariance, FlashTP, OpenEquivariance, eager} × {Allegro, MACE, NequIP, SevenNet}
on all primary systems. Report speedup and predicate overhead separately. Falsification: composed
speedup below 1.3× on the favourable systems.
11.7.6

E6 — Error correlation exponent

Measure γ in ε/n̄γ across systems by comparing predicted and actual aggregate error. Report all three
budget modes.
11.7.7

E7 — Drift and the zero-boundary mechanisms

100 ps NVE on four systems × {no mechanism, envelope switching, velocity rescaling} × four
tolerances. Validate Theorem 5.5 by checking that measured drift scales as predicted with K and
ε. Falsification: drift exceeding 10−2 meV atom−1 ps−1 with a mechanism enabled indicates the
conservativity argument is not realised in the implementation.
11.7.8

E8 — Stride ablation (the decisive comparison)

Frost vs. fixed-stride k ∈ {2, 5, 10, 25, 50, 100} at matched force error. Also: staggered vs. synchronised
refresh, reporting p50/p99 step latency. Falsification: if the Pareto front of stride-k matches Frost’s,
spatial adaptivity adds nothing and the contribution collapses to a reimplementation of multiple time
stepping.
11.7.9

E9 — Adversarial regimes

Proton transfer, melting Al, shock. Report φ and speedup where Frost is predicted to degrade. Success:
graceful degradation to ≥ 1.0× (never slower than baseline), verifying the admission-control fallback
of §8.
11.7.10

E10 — Memory and maximum system size

Largest system runnable on one A6000, Frost vs. baseline, with and without activation checkpointing.
Success: ≥ 1.5× larger.

F ROST: I NCREMENTAL GNN I NFERENCE FOR G EOMETRIC ROLLOUTS

11.7.11

37

E11 — Two-GPU scaling

Configurations R and S on 2×A6000 over PCIe. Compare against DistMLIP-style partitioning [13]
on the same hardware. Hypothesis: Frost’s PCIe traffic is φ -proportional and therefore far below
layer-wise partitioning.
11.7.12

E12 — Precision interaction

Cache precision {FP32, BF16, FP16} × compute precision {FP32, BF16}, following the protocol of [6].
Hypothesis: BF16 cache is free within the existing error budget.
11.7.13

E13 — Cross-generation validation

Repeat E1 and E5 on A100 and H100. Purpose: φ is hardware-independent (it is physics) but θ is not,
since kernel efficiency shifts the compute/bandwidth balance. This experiment tests whether Frost’s
benefit is an artefact of Ampere.
11.7.14

E14 — Physical fidelity suite

RDF, VDOS, Ds , elastic constants, phonons on twenty systems; Matbench Discovery relaxation at full
scale through TorchSim. Falsification: any observable outside the statistical error of the exact baseline.
