Optimality, Control, and Limits

Three additions strengthen the proposal in different directions: a proof that Frost’s predicate is not a
heuristic but the optimal policy under a stated model (§12.1); a closed-loop controller that replaces the
tolerance hyperparameter with an accuracy service-level objective (§12.2); and an explicit statement of
the performance ceiling, so the speedup claims are bounded from above by us rather than by a reviewer
(§12.3). A fourth subsection addresses a threat the earlier draft missed entirely: the correctness oracle
assumes NVE, but production runs are thermostatted (§12.4).

12.1

The predicate is Lagrangian-optimal

F ROST: I NCREMENTAL GNN I NFERENCE FOR G EOMETRIC ROLLOUTS

12.1.1

38

The allocation problem

Frost’s threshold rule (Equation 14) looks like a heuristic. It is not. Pose the per-step decision as
constrained optimisation: choose a refresh set H ⊆ E minimising cost subject to a total error budget,
min
H

∑

ce

e∈H

s.t.

∑

ηe ≤ εtot ,

(29)

e∈H
/

where ce is the recompute cost of edge e and ηe its current error contribution. This is a knapsack, and
the standard treatment is Lagrangian relaxation — the same device RDKV uses for bit allocation in
KV caches, solving a multiple-choice knapsack by bisection on the dual variable so that each unit
independently minimises wu εu (b) + λ b against a budget [25]. We claim no methodological novelty for
the technique. What we claim is the result:
Theorem 12.1 (The threshold predicate is optimal). Under a separable error model in which the total
error is ∑e∈H
ηe , the optimal solution to Equation (29) is a threshold rule on the benefit–cost ratio:
/
refresh e if and only if ηe /ce > λ ⋆ , where λ ⋆ is the shadow price of the error constraint.
Proof. The Lagrangian is ∑e∈H ce + λ (∑e∈H
ηe − εtot ). It separates over edges: edge e contributes
/
ce if refreshed and λ ηe otherwise. Refreshing is optimal iff ce < λ ηe , i.e. ηe /ce > 1/λ . Setting
λ ⋆ = 1/λ and applying complementary slackness at the budget gives the claim. The LP relaxation of a
knapsack of this form has O(1) integrality gap, so the greedy threshold rule is asymptotically exact as
|E | grows [24].
Corollary 12.2 (When uniform cost is assumed). If all edges have equal recompute cost — true within
an architecture, since every edge runs the same tensor product — the rule reduces to a threshold on
ηe alone. That is exactly Equation (14). Frost’s predicate is therefore the KKT-optimal policy, not an
approximation to it.
12.1.2

Where the assumption breaks, and what to do

Theorem 12.1 rests on separability: total error is the sum of per-edge errors. Two ways it fails, both of
which we can name and handle:
• Correlated errors. If neighbouring edges’ errors add coherently, the true total is superadditive and
the threshold under-refreshes. This is precisely the correlation exponent γ of §4.6; setting γ → 1
restores conservatism. E6 measures it.
• Non-uniform cost. Cost is uniform per edge within a layer but not across layers, and the first-order
cache makes refresh cost depend on whether the Jacobian is stored. Frost therefore implements the
general ratio form ηe /ce , with ce read from the offline cost table, and falls back to the pure threshold
when costs are uniform.
Stating the exact assumption under which the policy is optimal is more useful than claiming it is
optimal unconditionally, and it identifies E6 as a theory-validating experiment rather than a tuning
exercise.

12.2
12.2.1

Closed-loop accuracy control
What already exists

Closed-loop control of caching is not new. SoftCap formulates the runtime policy as a closed-loop
budget controller for diffusion transformers, maintaining a soft compute ceiling and a reference progress
profile, with a trajectory-drift observer supplying a local approximation-risk signal and the discrepancy
between reference and realised compute setting the risk threshold [26]. Related work observes that

F ROST: I NCREMENTAL GNN I NFERENCE FOR G EOMETRIC ROLLOUTS

39

runtime-triggered cache decisions make the number of model evaluations input-dependent, which makes
latency hard to control under a fixed deployment budget [23].
We cite this directly because Frost’s controller must be positioned against it.
12.2.2

What Frost can do that budget control cannot

SoftCap controls compute to a budget, using an estimated risk signal. Frost can control the dual quantity
— accuracy to a target — using a measured one. That inversion is possible only because Frost has two
ground-truth error signals that diffusion caching lacks:
1. The canary residual (§19.6): 0.1% of edges recomputed exactly every step, giving a direct, unbiased,
online measurement of the actual cache error — not a proxy for it.
2. The energy drift D: a physical invariant that integrates all approximation error over the trajectory.
12.2.3

The controller

The user specifies a target drift D⋆ (or a target force error). Frost adjusts log ε with a PI law:
log εk+1 = log εk − Kp ek − Ki ∑ e j ,

ek = log

j≤k

D̂k
,
D⋆

(30)

where D̂k is the drift estimated over a sliding window. Control in log ε rather than ε is deliberate: the
error–tolerance relation is approximately power-law (linear for the zeroth-order cache, quadratic for the
first-order cache by §19.1), so the loop is near-linear in log space and a fixed gain works across several
decades of ε.
12.2.4

Why this matters beyond convenience

• It removes the last hyperparameter. A user who does not know what Frost is can still specify “do
not drift more than 10−2 meV atom−1 ps−1 ”, which is a quantity MD practitioners already reason
about.
• It adapts within a trajectory. A simulation that begins in a quiescent solid and later undergoes a
phase transition needs different tolerances in each regime. A fixed ε must be set for the worst phase;
the controller need not be.
• It converts the safety argument into a guarantee that is enforced, not assumed. If the approximation degrades, the loop tightens ε automatically rather than producing a silently wrong trajectory.
E22 (§12.6) evaluates tracking accuracy, settling time, and stability across a phase transition.

12.3

The performance ceiling

A proposal that does not bound its own claims invites a reviewer to do it less charitably. Frost has a
hard ceiling and it is worth stating.
As φ → 0, cost does not go to zero. Aggregation still runs over all E edges every step, and the
first-order path additionally reads the cache to evaluate Equation (34). From Appendix F,
Smax = lim

is read as recovering 57% of the available headroom rather than as an unqualified number.

12.4

The thermostat problem

This is a genuine gap in the earlier design and we correct it here.
12.4.1

The problem

§2.4.1 presents energy drift as a free, sensitive, uncontested correctness oracle. That is true — in
the NVE ensemble. But production molecular dynamics overwhelmingly runs NVT or NPT, where a
thermostat deliberately exchanges energy with a reservoir. A Langevin or Berendsen thermostat will
silently absorb exactly the kind of spurious energy injection Frost’s oracle is meant to detect. Under a
strong thermostat, a badly broken Frost run and a correct one can look identical.
12.4.2

Three responses

(a) Use the extended Hamiltonian where one exists. Nosé–Hoover dynamics conserve an extended
quantity — the physical energy plus the thermostat’s kinetic and potential terms. Monitoring that
conserved extended Hamiltonian gives back exactly the oracle, with no loss of ensemble. Frost therefore
defaults to Nosé–Hoover for validation runs and reports extended-Hamiltonian drift. This is the
technically correct answer and it costs nothing.
(b) Interleave NVE probe segments. For stochastic thermostats (Langevin, stochastic velocity
rescaling) no conserved quantity exists. Frost periodically switches off the thermostat for a short NVE
segment — say 1 ps every 100 ps — and measures drift there. Cost is 1%; the segments are also
physically harmless, being standard practice for checking integrator settings.
(c) Fall back to the canary under all ensembles. The canary residual (§19.6) is ensembleindependent: it compares cached against exact evaluation on the same configuration, so no thermostat
can mask it. It is a weaker signal — it measures instantaneous force error rather than accumulated
trajectory error — but it is always available, and it is what the closed-loop controller of §12.2 uses when
no conserved quantity exists.

12.5

Latency predictability

Runtime-triggered caching makes the number of expensive evaluations input-dependent, which makes
per-step latency hard to bound [23]. Frost inherits this: φ varies step to step, so step time does too.
For throughput-oriented MD — 106 steps, wall-clock measured in ns/day — this does not matter,
and it is the regime we target. For real-time and interactive uses (robotics rollouts, interactive simulation)
it does. Frost therefore provides a latency-bounded mode: a hard cap φcap on the recompute set per

F ROST: I NCREMENTAL GNN I NFERENCE FOR G EOMETRIC ROLLOUTS

step, with edges admitted in order of ηe /ce — which by Theorem 12.1 is the optimal ordering — and
the remainder deferred to the next step. Error is bounded by carrying the deferred residual forward in
the error accounting, so the guarantee degrades gracefully rather than breaking.
We report p50 and p99 step latency in all speedup experiments, and evaluate the latency-bounded
mode in E23.

12.6

Additional experiments

E22 — Closed-loop control. Track a drift setpoint across four systems including one undergoing a
solid–liquid transition. Measure tracking error, settling time, overshoot, and stability of ε. Success:
drift within 2× of setpoint throughout, including across the transition, with no oscillation.
E23 — Latency-bounded mode. Sweep φcap ; report p50/p99 step latency and the resulting accumulated error against the unbounded configuration. Success: p99/p50 below 1.3 at a < 10% throughput
cost.
E24 — Thermostat oracle validation. Deliberately inject a known error (a mis-set ε) and verify that
each oracle in Table 18 detects it. Success: extended-Hamiltonian drift and NVE probes both detect
the injected fault; this experiment validates the methodology, not the system, and belongs in the paper
because it shows the oracle works before it is used to certify anything.
E25 — Optimality gap. Compare the threshold policy against an oracle that solves Equation (29)
exactly by dynamic programming on a small system. Success: gap below 2%, confirming Theorem 12.1
empirically and bounding the cost of the separability assumption.

13

Beyond Chemistry: Cross-Domain Generalisation

Frost has so far been presented as a molecular dynamics system. That framing understates it, and this
section corrects the scope. The mechanism requires nothing chemical — it requires a graph neural
network evaluated repeatedly on a slowly deforming geometry. Atomistic simulation is the instance
where the supporting conditions are strongest, not the only one where they hold.

13.1

The abstract condition

Frost applies wherever four properties coincide:
C1 Autoregressive geometric rollout. A GNN is evaluated repeatedly, each evaluation advancing a
configuration that becomes the next input.

F ROST: I NCREMENTAL GNN I NFERENCE FOR G EOMETRIC ROLLOUTS

42

C2 Geometry is the state. Node positions evolve; edges and edge features are functions of those
positions. This is what makes displacement a valid invalidation signal — it is the cause of feature
change, not a correlate.
C3 A principled error budget. Some externally validated quantity states how accurate the model
actually is, so the approximation tolerance is derived rather than tuned.
C4 A correctness oracle. Some measurable quantity reveals whether the approximation has broken the
computation.
The technical name for C1–C2 is Lagrangian rollout: the mesh or particle set moves with the
material, so the graph deforms rather than staying fixed. This is the class Frost addresses, and it contains
considerably more than molecular dynamics.

13.2
13.2.1

Learned physics simulators: the same problem
Graph Network Simulators (GNS)

GNS represents a physical system as particles with learned message passing, and generates long
trajectories by feeding predictions back as inputs. The relevant detail for us is stated in the original
paper’s own methods: for generating rollouts, on each timestep the graph’s edges are recomputed by a
nearest-neighbour algorithm to reflect the current particle positions, and the processor applies a stack of
graph network blocks whose complexity is linear in the edge count [19].
That is the same loop as molecular dynamics with a Verlet neighbour list, expressed in different
vocabulary. Particles move by a small increment per step; the radius graph is recomputed; edge features
are relative displacements; the processor runs M rounds of message passing whose cost is O(M|E |).
13.2.2

MeshGraphNets

MeshGraphNets operate on a deforming mesh: the encoder transforms the input mesh at time t into
a graph, adding extra world-space edges; the processor performs several rounds of message passing
along mesh edges and world edges, updating all node and edge embeddings; and the decoder extracts a
per-node acceleration used to advance the mesh to time t + 1 [18].
MeshGraphNets carry two edge types, and both are within Frost’s reach for different reasons:
• Mesh-space edges have fixed topology (the mesh connectivity does not change in the non-adaptive
setting) but position-dependent features — relative displacement in both mesh space and world space.
Their features deform even though their existence does not. Frost’s predicate applies to the features.
• World-space edges are radius-based and are added and removed as the geometry deforms, exactly
like a neighbour list. Frost’s generation-counter machinery (§8.2) handles them without modification.
13.2.3

Why the theory transfers verbatim

Proposition 13.1 (Domain independence of closure saturation). Theorem 3.2 makes no reference to
atoms, energies, forces, or equivariance. Its hypotheses are: a radius graph of uniform density ρ
with cutoff rc , M rounds of message passing, and independent dirty marks with probability p. Any
Lagrangian rollout GNN satisfies these hypotheses.
The consequence is that the central negative result — node-level caching is unworkable, edge-level
caching is required — is a statement about message-passing GNNs on radius graphs, and it holds for
GNS and MeshGraphNets with the same constants. Likewise the attenuated-closure construction of
Algorithm 1, the hierarchical predicate cascade of §19.2, and the first-order extrapolation of §19.1 are
all architecture-agnostic.

F ROST: I NCREMENTAL GNN I NFERENCE FOR G EOMETRIC ROLLOUTS

43

Table 19: Which Frost properties transfer to learned simulators. The energy-conservation oracle and the

No Boltzmann distribution to reweight

The budget argument is stronger outside chemistry

This is the finding that most surprised us, and it is the strongest reason to include a second domain.
In chemistry the tolerance derives from the model’s DFT force error — a number the practitioner
must look up. In learned simulators the tolerance derives from something better: the noise the model
was explicitly trained to tolerate.
GNS and MeshGraphNets are trained with input noise augmentation, precisely because rollout
inputs are known to be corrupted by the model’s own accumulated errors. The original GNS work
identifies this as central: the main determinants of long-term rollout performance are the number of
message-passing steps and the mitigation of error accumulation by corrupting training inputs with
noise [19], with subsequent work confirming that input-noise augmentation is critical to controlling
multi-step rollout error [16].
The consequence for Frost is direct. The training noise scale σtrain is a published hyperparameter
describing a perturbation magnitude the model was optimised to be robust against. Setting the caching
tolerance at or below σtrain is therefore not merely defensible — it introduces a perturbation of a kind
and magnitude the model has already been trained to absorb.
A budget the model was trained to tolerate
In MLIPs, the argument is “the approximation is below the model’s measured error.” In learned
simulators, it is stronger: “the approximation is below the noise magnitude the model was
explicitly trained to be robust to, and of the same character — a small perturbation of the input
geometry’s induced features.” The tolerance stops being a tuning parameter and becomes a
quantity read from the training configuration.

13.4

What is lost, stated plainly

The transfer is not free. Frost’s strongest correctness claim does not survive it.
Table 19 is the honest accounting. Roughly two-thirds of Frost transfers cleanly; the correctness machinery that we regard as the paper’s most distinctive contribution does not, because it is a consequence
of the physics rather than of the mechanism. This asymmetry is exactly why molecular dynamics
remains the flagship domain and the learned simulators are the generalisation study, rather than
the other way round. We state it in the paper in these terms.

F ROST: I NCREMENTAL GNN I NFERENCE FOR G EOMETRIC ROLLOUTS

44

For GNS and MeshGraphNets, correctness is instead assessed by the field’s own standard: rollout
error against the exact model over hundreds of steps, plus the domain metrics used in the original works
(particle MSE for GNS; RMSE and stress-field error for MeshGraphNets).

13.5

13.6.2

New experiments

E19 — Cross-domain redundancy. Repeat E1 on GNS (WaterDrop, Sand, Goop) and MeshGraphNets (FlagSimple, DeformingPlate, SphereDynamic), plus CylinderFlow as negative control. Report
φ (ε) with ε expressed as a fraction of the model’s training noise scale σtrain . Success: φ < 0.5 at
ε = σtrain /10 on at least DeformingPlate and Goop. Negative control: φ ≈ 1 on CylinderFlow.
E20 — Cross-domain speedup and rollout fidelity. End-to-end speedup, plus rollout error against
the exact model over 400 steps, using each dataset’s native metric. Falsification: rollout error exceeding
the exact model’s own error against ground truth. If Frost’s perturbation is smaller than the model’s
existing rollout drift, it is invisible in practice — and that is the claim to test.
E21 — Training-noise tolerance calibration. Sweep ε relative to σtrain across {0.01, 0.1, 0.5, 1, 2}.
Hypothesis: rollout quality degrades sharply only above σtrain , confirming that the training noise scale
is the natural tolerance and providing a defaulting rule requiring no per-model tuning.
13.6.3

Cost

Two to three weeks. Reference implementations and datasets are public; the models are small enough to
run in hours on one A6000; and Frost’s model shim is roughly 100 lines per architecture (§10). The
mechanism itself needs no modification, which is the point being demonstrated.

13.7

Consequence for the paper’s framing

With the cross-domain study, Frost is no longer a chemistry system. It is incremental GNN inference
for autoregressive geometric rollouts, evaluated across atomistic simulation, particle-based fluids and
granular media, and mesh-based solid and cloth mechanics — with molecular dynamics as the case study
where the correctness guarantees are strongest and a negative control confirming that the mechanism
depends on the property we claim it does.
This also resolves threat T9 (§18). The question “why should a systems venue care about chemistry”
does not arise when the evaluation spans three model families across two communities and the theory
section contains no physics at all.

14

Benchmarking Against the State of the Art

A systems paper in an application domain is judged on whether it engages that domain’s own evaluation
standards. This section specifies exactly which models, benchmarks, metrics, and protocols Frost is
measured against, taken from what the leading groups in each domain currently report.

14.1

The key framing: Frost is measured in the leaderboard’s own units

Matbench Discovery already reports computational cost alongside accuracy. Its metric set includes
Speed, Slowdown, VRAM, and RAM columns next to F1, RMSD, κSRME , and the Combined Performance
Score (CPS) [31]. This is unusually convenient, and it determines how we state the central claim.

F ROST: I NCREMENTAL GNN I NFERENCE FOR G EOMETRIC ROLLOUTS

46

The claim, in community-legible form
Frost moves a model left on the leaderboard’s speed axis without moving it down on any
accuracy axis. Concretely: identical F1, identical RMSD, identical κSRME , identical CPS —
within the benchmark’s own reported precision — at a reduced Slowdown and reduced VRAM. This
is falsifiable by public resubmission, requires no new metric of our invention, and is checkable by
anyone.
Reporting the claim this way avoids the standard failure of systems papers in scientific domains,
which is to invent a favourable metric. Every number we report on the MLIP side is one the community
already computes.

14.2

Current state of the art: models to benchmark against

Table 22 lists the models Frost is applied to or compared against, with published leaderboard figures at
the time of writing. Frost is applied to the gradient-based models — it requires forces to be −∇E —
and compared against the direct predictors as context.
14.2.1

A near-neighbour we must distinguish: DPA4’s edge cache

DPA4’s architecture description couples “a shared edge cache” with its geometry-informed embedding
and stacked equivariant interaction blocks [27]. The name is close enough to Frost’s that a reviewer will
ask, so we state the distinction plainly.
• DPA4’s edge cache is intra-forward-pass. It shares edge-wise intermediates across layers within a
single evaluation, an architectural optimisation that eliminates redundant recomputation inside one
call.
• Frost’s cache is inter-evaluation. It reuses edge quantities across successive timesteps of a trajectory,
and is a runtime mechanism applied to unmodified pretrained checkpoints.
The two are orthogonal and compose: an architecture with an intra-pass edge cache still recomputes
everything on the next timestep, which is exactly what Frost removes. We will make this distinction in
the paper’s introduction rather than burying it, and evaluate Frost on DPA4 to demonstrate composition.
14.2.2

Non-conservative models as a control

ORB-v2 and EquiformerV2 predict forces directly rather than as −∇E. Their κSRME values of 1.726 and
1.676 against 0.340 for eSEN illustrate what abandoning conservativity costs on derivative-sensitive
properties. We include one such model deliberately: it lets us show that Frost’s conservative-byconstruction design (§4.5) preserves a property these architectures have already given up, and it tests
whether Frost’s mechanism still delivers speedup when the conservativity argument is moot.

14.3
14.3.1

Benchmark suite
Why the DynaMat MD task matters most

The molecular dynamics task added to Matbench Discovery scores how faithfully MLIPs reproduce
finite-temperature AIMD observables — radial distribution functions, vibrational density of states,
pressure distributions, and single-point energy and force RMSEs [31]. Its reference set spans pure
metals, alloys, high-entropy alloys, transition-metal dichalcogenides, perovskites, and molecular crystals
from 293 to 1500 K [28].
This is precisely Frost’s workload, with precisely the fidelity metrics we independently proposed
in §11, maintained by the community rather than by us. We therefore adopt it as the primary fidelity
benchmark and drop our bespoke RDF/VDOS protocol in favour of it wherever the systems overlap.

F ROST: I NCREMENTAL GNN I NFERENCE FOR G EOMETRIC ROLLOUTS

47

Caveat we will state in the paper: the benchmark’s own documentation flags the MD task as
preliminary, with metrics and reference set still evolving and rankings to be treated as indicative [31].
We report DynaMat results as an equality check — Frost versus exact evaluation of the same model —
rather than as a ranking claim, which sidesteps the instability entirely.
14.3.2

The diatomic smoothness metrics are a trap we should walk into deliberately

The leaderboard tracks energy flips, energy jumps, force flips, force total variation, and force jumps
along diatomic dissociation curves [31]. These detect non-smoothness in the learned PES.
A naive caching scheme would fail these badly: swapping a cached value for a fresh one introduces
a discontinuity, which is exactly what a “jump” metric measures. Frost’s envelope-switching mechanism
(§5.3) makes cache transitions C1 , and this benchmark is the direct, external, community-maintained
test of whether that works. We will report it prominently, including in the ablation where switching
is disabled, because a mechanism that survives a test designed to catch it is more convincing than one
that is never tested.

14.4

Systems-side comparison

14.5

Cross-domain state of the art

14.6

On benchmark selection and Goodhart’s law

A recent survey of open questions in MLIP foundation models places benchmarks on a spectrum from
representational (probing intrinsic model properties such as parameter count, scaling, inference cost)
to pragmatic (mimicking practical situations, such as how long an MD run remains stable at elevated
temperature), and warns explicitly that pragmatic benchmarks influence the systems they measure —
Goodhart’s law [32].
Frost occupies an unusual and favourable position with respect to this concern. Frost is not
optimising any accuracy benchmark; it is required to leave every accuracy benchmark unchanged.
Its optimisation target is the cost axis, and its accuracy obligation is an equality constraint against
the exact evaluation of the same model. There is therefore no benchmark for Frost to overfit — a
model that games a metric under Frost would game it equally without Frost. We think this is worth
one sentence in the paper, because it removes a class of reviewer suspicion that ordinarily attaches to
accuracy-versus-speed tradeoffs.

15
15.1

Scaling, Statistics, and Ablation
Frost improves with system size

Most acceleration techniques degrade at scale. Frost should improve, and the reason is structural enough
to prove.
Definition 15.1 (Hot region). Let a configuration contain a hot region Ωhot — a reaction site, defect,
crack tip, interface, or contact patch — in which the per-step geometric perturbation exceeds the
invalidation threshold with probability phot , and a bulk region in which it does so with probability
pbulk ≪ phot .
Theorem 15.2 (Dirty fraction is non-increasing in system size). Let Nhot (N) be the number of atoms in
the hot region for a system of N atoms. Then
φ (N) = pbulk + phot − pbulk

Proof. Immediate from the definition by linearity of expectation. Monotonicity follows because

Corollary 15.3 (Speedup increases with system size). Since S is strictly decreasing in φ (Equation 23) and φ (N) is non-increasing, S (N) is non-decreasing. Frost’s benefit grows with system size,
approaching the ceiling Smax of §12.3 as N → ∞ for any sub-extensive hot region.
Why this matters for the pitch
The community’s stated blocker is nanosecond-scale MD of 104 –106 atoms [63]. Theorem 15.2
says Frost is most effective precisely there. This is the opposite of the usual scaling story for
approximation techniques, and it is a direct consequence of physics being local: interesting things
happen in small regions, and those regions do not grow proportionally with the box.
E26 (§15.4) tests the predicted exponents directly by sweeping system size at fixed defect structure
— a falsifiable prediction of a specific power law, not a vague claim that bigger is better.

15.2

Statistical methodology

Twenty-six experiments invite both selective reporting and multiple-comparison problems. We state the
protocol in advance.
The commitment that matters most is the fourth row. It is easy to produce a large speedup against
ASE-eager MLIP inference and misleading to present it as the headline. Our primary comparison is
always against the strongest available baseline running the same kernel backend.

15.3

Ablation matrix

Reviewers ask which component does the work. The design is modular precisely so that each can be
disabled independently.
Two rows carry disproportionate weight. Fixed stride vs. Frost-0 isolates the paper’s central claim
— that spatial adaptivity beats temporal alone. Mesh-space-only isolates how much benefit requires no
approximation whatsoever.

15.4

Additional experiments

E26 — Scaling exponents. Sweep N from 103 to 105 at fixed defect structure (single vacancy; a
dislocation line; a free surface), and fit φ (N) − pbulk ∝ N −α . Success: measured α within 0.15 of the
predicted {1, 2/3, 1/3}. Falsification: φ flat or increasing in N, which would invalidate Theorem 15.2’s
locality assumption and remove the strongest scaling argument.
E27 — Full ablation. Table 27 across four systems and three architectures. Success: every enabled
component contributes positively on at least one system; any component that never helps is removed
from the paper.

F ROST: I NCREMENTAL GNN I NFERENCE FOR G EOMETRIC ROLLOUTS

49

E28 — Warm-up and steady state. Time to reach steady-state φ from a cold cache; cost of the
first N steps. Purpose: short simulations may never amortise the warm-up, and we should know the
crossover trajectory length rather than let a user discover it.

16

Cross-Domain Architecture and Adaptation

§13 argued that Frost’s mechanism is domain-independent. This section specifies the adaptation
concretely for each target architecture: what is cached, what the invalidation predicate is, where the
closure boundary falls, and what the domain-specific sensitivity surrogate looks like. The claim that the
shim is roughly one hundred lines per architecture is only credible if we can say exactly what those
lines do.

16.1

The adaptation interface

Frost requires a model to expose three things. Everything else is inherited.

16.2

Adaptation A — Equivariant interatomic potentials

Covered in §4; restated here for comparison.
• Geometry: ri j ∈ R3 , i.e. d = 3.
• Cached unit: gi j = R(ri j ) ⊗CG Yℓm (r̂i j ), the Clebsch–Gordan tensor product of radial basis and
spherical harmonics.
• Closure boundary: zero at layer 1 (Prop. 3.4); attenuated beyond. Zero at all layers for Allegro.
• Sensitivity Ŝ: tabulated by (Zi , Z j , binned ri j ); dominated by the radial envelope derivative.
• Tolerance source: published validation force RMSE, 20 meV/Å to 60 meV/Å.

16.3

Adaptation B — Graph Network Simulators

GNS follows encode–process–decode. The encoder deliberately masks absolute positions and encodes
only relative displacement between particles into edge features [19], which is precisely the structure
Frost needs.
• Geometry: (ri j , ∥ri j ∥) ∈ R4 .
(0)

• Cached unit: the encoder edge embedding ei j = MLPenc (ri j , ∥ri j ∥). This is a pure function of edge
geometry — no node state enters — so Proposition 3.4 applies verbatim.
• Closure boundary: zero at the encoder. The processor blocks update edges as e′ = φe (e, vi , v j ),
which does consume node state, so blocks 1. . .M require attenuated closure exactly as MACE’s layer
2 does.
• Sensitivity Ŝ: no species table; instead binned by ∥ri j ∥ and particle-type pair (water/sand/goop/boundary). Fitted by the same Hutchinson-probe procedure of Appendix B.
• Tolerance source: the training noise scale σtrain , a published hyperparameter (§13).
• Domain wrinkle: node features include velocity history (typically five previous velocities), which
changes every step regardless of position. This is why caching must stop at the encoder — and it is a
concrete instance of why node-level caching is hopeless in this class of model.

F ROST: I NCREMENTAL GNN I NFERENCE FOR G EOMETRIC ROLLOUTS

16.4

50

Adaptation C — MeshGraphNets

MeshGraphNets carry two edge sets and, uniquely among our targets, one of them has a permanently
cacheable component.
• Mesh edges. Features are the relative displacement in mesh space (ui j , ∥ui j ∥) concatenated with the
relative displacement in world space (xi j , ∥xi j ∥).
• World edges. Radius-based, world-space displacement only; created and destroyed as the geometry
deforms, exactly like a neighbour list.
A free win specific to MeshGraphNets
In the non-adaptive setting the reference mesh does not deform, so the mesh-space relative
displacement ui j is exactly constant for the entire rollout. Half of every mesh-edge feature
vector can therefore be computed once at step zero and reused with zero error, permanently —
no predicate, no tolerance, no bound required. This is not an approximation; it is an algebraic
identity that current implementations recompute 103 –104 times per trajectory.
The remaining adaptation:
• Cached unit: the mesh-edge and world-edge encoder embeddings. The mesh-space half is cached
unconditionally; the world-space half is cached under the predicate.
• Closure boundary: zero at the encoder; attenuated through the processor, which passes messages
along both edge sets.
• Sensitivity Ŝ: binned by world-space distance and node-type pair (interior, boundary, obstacle,
handle).
• Generation counters handle world-edge churn without modification (§8.2); mesh edges never churn
in the non-adaptive setting and can use a static key.
• Adaptive remeshing — MeshGraphNets’ optional sizing-field remesher — breaks the static-mesh
identity above. Frost detects a remesh event and invalidates wholesale, treating it exactly as a
keyframe. Cost is one full step per remesh, which is rare by design.

16.5

Side-by-side

16.6

Cross-domain baselines

The mesh-space-only ablation is worth emphasising. If a meaningful share of the MeshGraphNets
speedup comes from the exactly-constant mesh-space features, then that portion is lossless — no
tolerance, no error, no bound needed. Reporting it separately lets a reader see how much benefit is free
and how much is bought with approximation.

16.7

Cross-domain metrics

The divergence step deserves emphasis as the headline fidelity metric. Learned simulators already
accumulate rollout error — that is why they are trained with noise augmentation. The correct question is
therefore not “does Frost introduce error” but “does Frost introduce error that is visible above the error
already present”. Framing it this way is both more honest and more favourable, and it is the framing the
domain’s own literature uses.

F ROST: I NCREMENTAL GNN I NFERENCE FOR G EOMETRIC ROLLOUTS

17

51

Related Work

We organise related work into five strands and state, for each, exactly what Frost inherits and where
it differs. We adopt the view that a research contribution need not be novel in every component —
PagedAttention reused continuous batching; FlashAttention reused online softmax — but must be
clearly separable in its load-bearing ideas.

17.1

Temporal redundancy in MLIP-driven dynamics

This is the closest prior art and deserves the most precise treatment.
Multiple time stepping. The RESPA family [58] splits forces by component and evaluates expensive
components less often. The modern MLIP instantiation is DMTS/DMTS-NC [9, 50], which couples an
accurate conservative potential to a distilled surrogate producing non-conservative forces within a duallevel RESPA formalism. Difference: MTS splits by force component and treats every atom identically
on a fixed stride; Frost splits by spatial region and edge, adaptively. More importantly, DMTS-NC
deliberately enters the non-conservative regime and must recover stability through architectural priors;
Frost’s Proposition 4.3 never leaves it. The known instabilities of MTS [7, 43] are therefore not
inherited.
BoostMD. BoostMD uses node embeddings from a reference MLIP at previous time steps to predict
energies and forces for subsequent configurations, letting a faster model run between less frequent
full evaluations [8]. This is the single most similar prior work and the comparison must be explicit.
Differences: (i) BoostMD requires training a boost model; Frost is training-free and works with any
pretrained checkpoint. (ii) BoostMD applies uniformly to all atoms on a fixed schedule; Frost is per-edge
and adaptive. (iii) BoostMD offers no error bound; Frost’s is derived from the model’s own validated
error. (iv) The two compose: Frost’s caching could accelerate BoostMD’s reference evaluations.
Trajectory generation. TrajCast bypasses force evaluation entirely, directly updating positions and
velocities with autoregressive equivariant networks [57]. This is a more radical departure that abandons
the potential energy surface; Frost accelerates the conventional formulation without changing its
semantics. The two are not substitutes — TrajCast trades physical guarantees for speed, Frost preserves
them.
Architectural pruning. Zhang et al. remove low-contribution message-passing layers from atomistic
foundation models, motivated by over-smoothing and quantified via linear CKA between layers [63].
Frost uses their measurement to justify attenuated closure, and the two compose: a pruned model has
fewer layers and hence smaller closure, making Frost more effective.

17.2

Caching in iterative neural inference

Diffusion models face structurally the same problem — an iterative process with smoothly varying
intermediate states — and the literature there is mature. DeepCache reuses U-Net features across
timesteps; First-Block Cache uses the first block’s output as a proxy; SpectralCache identifies temporal,
depth, and feature axes of non-uniformity [54]; ERTACache decomposes cumulative error into feature
shift and step amplification with closed-form residual linearisation [14]; ReCache learns caching
schedules via REINFORCE [52]; and X-Slim exploits redundancy across timesteps, blocks, and
tokens with a dual-threshold push-then-polish controller, requiring full inference once a critical line is
crossed [62].
Most directly relevant to Frost’s design, SenCache formalises caching error through the model’s
output sensitivity to input perturbations and uses that sensitivity to select reuse points adaptively per

F ROST: I NCREMENTAL GNN I NFERENCE FOR G EOMETRIC ROLLOUTS

52

sample, explicitly to replace hand-tuned heuristics [20]. Frost’s sensitivity surrogate Ŝ (§4.3) is the same
idea, arrived at independently and instantiated differently: the perturbation is a physical displacement
rather than a latent-space one, and Ŝ is an upper envelope fitted offline so the predicate is sound rather
than merely accurate. That SenCache reaches an analogous conclusion in an unrelated domain is, we
think, evidence the principle is right.
Frost borrows this skeleton openly — the keyframe reset is X-Slim’s “critical line”, the spatial axis
is its token axis, and the Taylor extrapolation of §19.1 has a direct precursor in TaylorSeer [17]. Three
differences make the transplant non-trivial:
1. The invalidation signal is physical, not heuristic. Diffusion caching uses learned or hand-crafted
error indicators; Frost uses atomic displacement, which is the cause of the feature change rather than
a correlate of it.
2. The tolerance is externally validated. Diffusion quality is judged by FID or perception; MLIP
error is measured against DFT and published with the checkpoint.
3. Correctness has a conservation law. No diffusion caching paper can prove anything analogous to
Theorem 5.5, because there is no conserved quantity to bound.

17.3

Acceleration of learned physics simulators

The GNS and MeshGraphNets acceleration literature is entirely architectural or partitioning-based, not
caching-based. BSMS-GNN introduces bi-stride multi-scale pooling to reduce message-passing cost
while preserving rollout stability [21]; X-MeshGraphNet partitions large graphs with halo regions so that
message passing across partitions is equivalent to whole-graph processing [22]; and subspace reduction
via PCA or autoencoders trains surrogates on lower-dimensional representations [16]. NVIDIA’s
PhysicsNeMo additionally applies gradient checkpointing and host offloading to fit deep processors in
memory [22] — the same inference-time use of a training technique that we noted for MLIPs in §2.1.
None of these exploits temporal redundancy between consecutive rollout steps. To our knowledge
no caching or incremental-computation approach has been applied to GNS or MeshGraphNets rollouts
at all, despite the loop structure being identical to the one Frost targets in molecular dynamics. §13
makes that transfer.

17.4

Atomistic simulation systems

TorchSim rewrote simulation primitives in PyTorch and introduced batching across systems of different
sizes with automatic memory management [56]; NVIDIA’s ALCHEMI Batched MD NIM productised
dynamic batching of concurrent MD requests [1], and the ALCHEMI Toolkit moved neighbour-list
construction and integration into PyTorch [2]. DistMLIP introduced graph-partitioned multi-GPU
inference preserving backpropagation intermediates [13]; Xia and Jiang gave a communication-minimal
parallel MPNN algorithm reaching 108 atoms [61]; Park et al. benchmarked uMLIP inference throughput
across accelerated backends [49]; and the mlip/mlip v2 libraries provide JAX-based end-to-end
workflows with dedicated equivariant kernels [44, 45].
Every one of these reduces the cost of performing forward–backward passes or of performing many
concurrently. None reduces the number of message computations performed. Frost is orthogonal to
all of them, sits above them in the stack (Figure 3), and is evaluated on top of them rather than against
them.

17.5

Equivariant kernel acceleration

cuEquivariance [10], FlashTP [15], OpenEquivariance [47], and e3j [45] accelerate the Clebsch–Gordan
tensor product. Benoit systematically evaluated reduced-precision execution policies for MACE [6].
These make each tensor product cheaper; Frost decides which to evaluate. Composition is multiplicative
and is measured in E5 and E12.

F ROST: I NCREMENTAL GNN I NFERENCE FOR G EOMETRIC ROLLOUTS

17.6

53

Incremental and approximate computation

The abstraction Frost imports is decades old. Self-adjusting computation and incremental computation
maintain outputs under input changes by tracking dependencies; interval arithmetic and abstract
interpretation propagate bounds rather than values. Approximate computing trades accuracy for
efficiency with a quality knob. Frost’s contribution is not these ideas but the observation that MLIPdriven MD is an unusually favourable substrate for them: the dependency structure is explicit (the
neighbour graph), the input perturbation is physically bounded (thermal motion), the quality knob is
externally calibrated (published force RMSE), and correctness has a conservation-law oracle.
The closest systems-community analogue is the shift from recomputing a prompt every token to
caching keys and values in LLM serving, and then from allocating that cache contiguously to paging
it [40]. Frost’s claim is structurally parallel: MD already caches the graph; it should also cache the
features on it.

17.7

Uncertainty quantification and active learning

A large literature reduces the cost of uncertainty estimation in MLIPs — evidential deep learning [60],
shallow ensembles, gradient-based uncertainty, D-optimality and MaxVol [51], and Gaussian-process
variance in FLARE [59]. These are algorithmic responses to a cost problem, and we note them because
they demonstrate the community’s willingness to accept principled approximation when the error is
bounded. Frost is not a UQ method, but it inherits the community norm that approximation is acceptable
when quantified.

18

Threats to Validity

We enumerate these because a proposal that hides its failure modes is not a research plan.

18.1

Threats to the central mechanism

T1 — Insufficient redundancy (severity: fatal). If φ is high at every physically defensible ε, there
is nothing to cache. This is the whole bet. Mitigation: E1 tests it first, in two weeks, with no code
written beyond a logger. Residual risk: moderate. The physics argument is strong for stiff condensed
phases and weak for liquids.
T2 — Attenuation does not contract (severity: major). If β ≥ 1, deep message-passing architectures
are out of reach. Mitigation: Allegro and layer-1 MACE results stand independently; the ordering of
the evaluation ensures a publishable result before this risk is taken. Residual risk: moderate.
T3 — Predicate overhead dominates (severity: major). The predicate touches every edge every step.
If it costs a substantial fraction of the tensor product it saves, the win evaporates at low φ . Mitigation:
fusion with the neighbour traversal (Listing 2); measured explicitly in E5. Residual risk: low — the
predicate is O(E) bandwidth against O(EF 2 ) compute.
T4 — Fixed-stride caching is already good enough (severity: fatal to the contribution). If stride-k
matches Frost’s Pareto front, spatial adaptivity buys nothing. Mitigation: E8 is designed as the decisive
comparison and is run early, not late. Residual risk: moderate. The counter-argument is that stride-k
must be set for the worst atom in the system, so a single reacting site forces a short stride globally —
which is exactly the situation Frost is designed for. But this must be shown, not asserted.

F ROST: I NCREMENTAL GNN I NFERENCE FOR G EOMETRIC ROLLOUTS

18.2

54

Threats to correctness

T5 — Silent cache corruption. A key collision or a mismatched entry after a neighbour rebuild
would corrupt forces without an obvious symptom. Mitigation: the ε = 0 bitwise differential test (§10.6)
exercises the full path with the approximation removed; nightly NVE canaries integrate error over time.
T6 — Misplaced detach. If the cached tensors are not correctly detached, the backward pass silently
computes a different gradient and Proposition 4.3 does not hold. Mitigation: finite-difference gradient
e
check on E.
T7 — Ergodicity and sampling bias. Even with bounded force error, systematic bias could shift the
sampled ensemble. Drift bounds energy but not distribution. Mitigation: E14’s transport and structural
observables (Ds , RDF) are precisely the quantities sensitive to systematic bias; phonons test second
derivatives.

18.3

Threats to significance

T8 — The workload might not matter. If practitioners run few, very large simulations rather than
long ones, throughput may not be the binding constraint. Assessment: low risk. The community states
the constraint explicitly [63], and nanosecond-scale MD of 104 –106 atoms is the stated target of every
foundation-model paper we cite.
T9 — Venue fit. MLSys reviewers may read “molecular dynamics” as chemistry. Mitigation: frame
as GNN inference with a mandatory backward pass, evaluate on systems metrics (step time, memory,
scaling), and cite the DFT-replacement motivation once rather than dwelling on it. Precedent exists:
MLSys and ASPLOS have accepted scientific-computing systems work, and the framing here — caching,
invalidation, closure, scheduling — is squarely a systems vocabulary.
T10 — Concurrent work. TorchSim and NVIDIA ALCHEMI are actively developed and well
resourced. Mitigation: build on TorchSim rather than against it, so that their progress strengthens rather
than threatens the result; and prioritise the characterisation (E1–E4), which is the durable contribution
regardless of who ships the runtime.

19

Advanced Mechanisms: Engineering the Risks Away

§18 enumerated ten threats. Four of them — insufficient redundancy, predicate overhead, sampling bias,
and the fixed-stride baseline — admit concrete design responses rather than mere measurement. This
section presents them. Two, in particular (§19.1 and §19.4), are substantial enough that we regard them
as part of the contribution rather than as contingency.

19.1

T1 — First-order cache extrapolation

Read §6 first
This subsection was originally written presenting the first-order cache as an enhancement over a
zeroth-order baseline. Theorem 6.1 shows that framing is wrong: zeroth-order caching deletes
the force contribution of every cached edge, so the first-order cache is the minimum admissible
design rather than an optimisation. We have kept the original exposition because the lifetime
analysis below is correct and load-bearing — only its status changed, from “worth the memory”
to “required”.

F ROST: I NCREMENTAL GNN I NFERENCE FOR G EOMETRIC ROLLOUTS

19.1.1

55

The problem restated

A zeroth-order cache holds gi j constant at its value at t0 . The induced error grows linearly in displacement,

Table 31 quantifies what the mandatory Jacobian buys. Had a zeroth-order cache been admissible,
its validity radius at the operating point of Appendix Q would be 1.8 × 10−3 Å — comparable to a
single timestep of thermal motion. The first-order cache reaches 6.1 × 10−2 Å, comparable to the entire
vibrational amplitude of a stiff solid at 300 K, so an edge in a harmonic well may never invalidate at all:
its excursion never leaves the linearised region. Since the Jacobian is required regardless (Theorem 6.1),
this reach is obtained at no marginal cost — the memory was already spent on correctness.
19.1.3

Why conservativity survives

This is the part that makes the fix admissible rather than merely attractive.
Proposition 19.1 (First-order caching preserves exact conservativity). With ĝ defined by Equation (34),
the surrogate energy Ee of Equation (15) remains a smooth, single-valued function of R on the refresh
interval. Hence F = −∇Ee is exactly conservative and Proposition 4.3 holds unchanged.
Proof. ḡi j , Ji j , and rref
i j are constants on the interval. Equation (34) is therefore an affine — hence
smooth — function of ri j , which is itself a smooth function of R. Substituting a smooth function of
R for another smooth function of R leaves Ee smooth in R. The argument of Proposition 4.3 applies
verbatim.
Remark 19.2 (Prior art, stated plainly). Taylor extrapolation of cached features is not new. TaylorSeer
applies Taylor-series expansion over multi-step feature histories to predict future features in diffusion
transformers, and is a recognised technique in that literature [17]. Frost’s version differs in three respects
that matter here, and we claim only those. First, the derivative is analytic, obtained from the backward
pass that already computes forces (Appendix D), rather than estimated by finite differences over a
history buffer — which removes both the extra storage of past states and the smoothness assumption

F ROST: I NCREMENTAL GNN I NFERENCE FOR G EOMETRIC ROLLOUTS

56

on the feature trajectory that LearniBridge identifies as TaylorSeer’s weak point [17]. Second, the
expansion is in physical coordinates rather than in timestep index, so the invalidation predicate and the
extrapolation share a single variable. Third, and most importantly, Proposition 19.1: in this setting the
extrapolation preserves an exact conservation law, which has no analogue in diffusion caching because
there is no conserved quantity there to preserve.
Remark 19.3 (Why conservativity survives a more aggressive approximation). It would be natural to
assume that a more aggressive approximation must weaken the correctness guarantee. It does not, and
the reason is structural: Frost’s guarantee comes from Ee being some smooth function of position, not
from it being close to Etot . Replacing a constant with an affine function keeps it smooth. The first-order
cache is therefore better on both axes — more accurate and longer lived — with the sole cost being
memory.
19.1.4

Cost

• Memory: 4×. Storing Ji j requires three additional tensors of the same shape as gi j (one per Cartesian
component). This is the real price. §19.1.5 gives three ways to reduce it.
• Compute at refresh: near zero. Ji j is the derivative of the radial basis and spherical harmonics with
respect to edge geometry — which the backward pass already computes to produce forces. Frost
intercepts and stores it rather than recomputing.
• Compute per step: one fused multiply–add per cached element. Evaluating Equation (34) is an
axpy over the cache. It is bandwidth-bound and, at 4× the read volume, is the second real price.
§19.1.5 discusses when it is not worth paying.
19.1.5

Managing the memory cost — and which schemes are inadmissible

The Jacobian is mandatory, so its 3× storage overhead is the price of admission. Four reduction schemes
were considered. Two of them fail for exactly the reason Theorem 6.1 identifies, and we record them
because the failure is instructive and because an implementer would otherwise reach for them.
(a) Radial-only Jacobian — INADMISSIBLE. Storing only ∂ g/∂ ri j (a scalar derivative, hence
2× total) discards the angular term of Equation (40). The retained derivative is then the projection
J r̂i j r̂⊤
i j , so ∂ ĝ/∂ ri j has zero component transverse to the bond. The transverse force contribution of
every cached edge is annihilated — the same failure as the zeroth-order cache, restricted to two of three
directions. Since bond bending is precisely the soft mode that dominates low-frequency dynamics, this
is not a tolerable approximation. Rejected.
(b) Low-rank truncation below k = 3 — INADMISSIBLE. Ji j maps R3 → Rnirrep and therefore has
rank at most 3 exactly (Appendix D). Storing it as a 3 × nirrep factorisation is not a compression — it is
the exact representation, and it is what “3× overhead” already means. Truncating to k < 3 deletes an
entire force direction per edge, again reproducing the Theorem 6.1 failure. Rejected as a compression;
retained as the correct storage layout.
(c) Reduced precision for J — ADMISSIBLE, and the default. The Jacobian need only be accurate
to the force tolerance, which is four orders of magnitude above FP32 resolution. Storing J in BF16 while
keeping g in FP32 gives 1 + 3(0.5) = 2.5× rather than 4×, and the induced force error is a bounded
relative perturbation of the derivative — a genuine perturbation, not a deletion. BF16 is preferred over
FP16 because its 8-bit exponent matches FP32’s dynamic range, which matters since tensor-product
outputs span several orders of magnitude across irrep orders.

F ROST: I NCREMENTAL GNN I NFERENCE FOR G EOMETRIC ROLLOUTS

57

(d) Envelope-weighted tiering — ADMISSIBLE. Edge contributions are weighted by the radial
envelope wi j , which vanishes with vanishing derivative at rc . For edges near the cutoff, both the value
and the derivative contribute negligibly, so a coarser — or even absent — Jacobian introduces an error
bounded by wi j , which is small by construction. Since edge count grows as r2 while w decays to zero,
this concentrates storage exactly where it matters. Crucially, this is admissible where (a) and (b) are
not: the error is bounded by a quantity we can compute and that tends to zero, rather than being an
unbounded deletion.
Remark 19.4 (The analysis earns its keep). Schemes (a) and (b) are the two an engineer would reach
for first, and both look obviously safe under an energy-error analysis: the discarded components are
small in value. They are unsafe because the quantity that matters is a derivative, and a small value
contribution can carry the entire derivative in a direction. This is the same mistake, in miniature, that
Theorem 6.1 corrects at the top level — which is some evidence that the C1 framing is the right one to
reason in throughout.

19.2

T3 — Hierarchical and lazy predicate evaluation

The predicate touches all E edges every step. Two mechanisms remove most of that work, both imported
directly from techniques MD engines already use one level down.
19.2.1

Velocity-bounded lazy testing

This is the Verlet skin argument applied to the cache. If the maximum atomic speed in the system is

The predicate cost falls from E tests per step to E/k̄, where k̄ is the mean deferral. With the firstorder cache extending Rvalid by a factor of 33, k̄ becomes large and the predicate becomes negligible —

vmax is obtained by a single reduction over velocities, which the thermostat already performs.
19.2.2

Hierarchical cell-level testing

Reuse the neighbour list’s spatial cell decomposition. For each cell c, maintain δc = maxi∈c ∥ri − rref
i ∥.
For an edge between cells c1 , c2 , the triangle inequality gives ∥∆ri j ∥ ≤ δc1 + δc2 . If Smax (δc1 + δc2 ) <
εedge , every edge between those cells is provably clean and none need be tested.
Figure 12 shows the resulting cascade. Every level is a sound filter — it may declare a clean edge
dirty (costing efficiency) but never a dirty edge clean (costing correctness). This means the hierarchy
can be tuned, disabled, or replaced without any correctness review, which is a valuable property for a
component that will be optimised repeatedly.

19.3

T4 — Dominance over fixed-stride caching by construction

The most dangerous threat to the contribution (as opposed to the system) is that a fixed stride captures
the same benefit. We remove the risk of Frost losing to stride, leaving only the empirical question of by
how much it wins.
Proposition 19.5 (Frost’s configuration space contains fixed-stride caching). Setting ε = ∞ (predicate
never fires), τmax = k (age trigger at k steps), and disabling staggering yields exactly fixed-stride-k
caching.

F ROST: I NCREMENTAL GNN I NFERENCE FOR G EOMETRIC ROLLOUTS

58

Proof. With ε = ∞ the geometric predicate of Equation (14) never triggers, so the only refresh source
is the age trigger. Unstaggered, all edges share phase and refresh simultaneously every k steps. That is
the definition of fixed-stride caching.
Three consequences:
1. Frost’s optimum is at least stride’s optimum. Since stride is a point in Frost’s configuration space,
maxFrost ≥ maxstride over any objective. The paper cannot report Frost losing.
2. Stride becomes an ablation, not a competitor. E8 is reframed: it measures the gap between a point
in the configuration space and its optimum, which is a cleaner scientific question than a head-to-head.
3. The explanatory statistic is identified in advance. The gap is governed by the coefficient of
variation of the per-edge refresh interval, CV = στ /τ̄. If CV = 0, every edge wants the same interval
and stride is optimal. Frost’s win is a monotone function of CV, so we can predict where Frost helps
before running the speedup benchmark, and report CV per system as the mechanistic explanation.
This converts a possible negative result into a characterisation.

19.4
19.4.1

T7 — Importance reweighting for unbiased ensembles
The problem restated

Theorem 5.5 bounds energy drift, and E14 checks observables empirically. Neither proves that the
e
sampled ensemble is unbiased. Frost samples from ∝ e−β E , not e−β Etot . A small but systematic
difference could shift a computed transport coefficient or a free-energy difference without producing
any drift signature at all — because the dynamics remain perfectly conservative under the wrong
Hamiltonian.
This is, in our judgement, the most intellectually serious threat in the whole proposal, and it deserves
a fix rather than a caveat.
19.4.2

The fix

Frost’s surrogate is not a black box: Ee is a known, evaluable quantity, and Etot can be computed exactly
on demand. Standard reweighting therefore applies. For any observable A,
A e−β (Etot −E)
e

⟨A⟩Etot =

e
e−β (Etot −E)

Ee

(38)

Ee

where ⟨·⟩Ee denotes an average over the Frost trajectory. The weights require Etot — one exact evaluation
per retained frame, not per step. With frames retained every m = 100 steps, the overhead is 1%.
19.4.3

Why this is cheap and why it works here

• The overlap is excellent by construction. Reweighting fails when the two distributions overlap
e is bounded by the tolerance — that is the entire design — so β ∆E ≪ 1 and
poorly. Here |Etot − E|
the weights are near unity. This is the opposite of the usual reweighting regime, where one reweights
between genuinely different Hamiltonians and the estimator degenerates.
• The diagnostic is standard and free. The effective sample size Neff = (∑ w)2 / ∑ w2 quantifies
estimator degradation. Reporting Neff /N per run turns “is the bias material?” from a worry into a
number printed in the log.
• It composes with the drift bound. Theorem 5.5 controls the trajectory; Equation (38) corrects the
statistics. Together they cover both failure modes.

F ROST: I NCREMENTAL GNN I NFERENCE FOR G EOMETRIC ROLLOUTS

59

Remark 19.6 (A stronger claim becomes available). With reweighting enabled, Frost’s claim strengthens from “the error is small” to “equilibrium averages are asymptotically unbiased, with a measured
effective sample size”. That is a qualitatively different guarantee, and it is one that neither BoostMD
nor DMTS-NC can offer, because neither exposes a surrogate energy whose exact counterpart can be
evaluated on the same configuration.
19.4.4

Limitations, stated

Reweighting corrects equilibrium averages. It does not correct dynamical quantities computed from
time correlation functions — diffusion coefficients from the mean-squared displacement, vibrational
spectra from the velocity autocorrelation — because those depend on the trajectory itself, not only
on the sampled distribution. For those, we fall back on E14’s direct comparison and on the tolerance
argument. We say so in the paper rather than overclaiming the scope of Equation (38).

19.5

T2 — Layer-wise budget and the exact-deep split

If E3 returns β ≥ 1 and attenuated closure does not contract, we do not abandon message-passing
architectures. Two graceful degradations:
Layer-wise budget allocation. Rather than a uniform εt , solve for the allocation minimising total
recomputation subject to the total error budget:


min ∑ φt (εt ) ct s.t. ∑ ∏ αu n̄w̄ εt ≤ ε.
(39)
{εt } t
