u>t

This is a small convex problem solved once at startup. When attenuation is weak, the optimiser naturally
assigns almost all budget to layer 1 and near-zero to deep layers — automatically recovering the
exact-deep split below rather than requiring a separate code path.
Exact-deep / cached-shallow. Cache layer 1 (where closure is provably zero) and compute layers
2 . . . M exactly. The speedup ceiling drops to θ1 , the layer-1 share of cost — roughly 0.45 for MACE
(Table 1) — giving up to 1.8× at φ → 0 per Table 5. Modest, but real, guaranteed by Proposition 3.4,
and requiring no assumption about attenuation whatsoever.
The worst case is not zero
Even under complete failure of attenuated closure, Frost retains: (i) full applicability to strictly
local architectures such as Allegro, where closure is identically zero; (ii) layer-1 caching for
all message-passing architectures; and (iii) the first-order cache, hierarchical predicate, and
reweighting machinery, all of which are independent of closure. The proposal has a floor.

19.6

T5/T6 — Cache integrity engineering

Silent corruption is the failure mode that would waste the most time. Four guards beyond the tests of
§10.6:
1. Generation counters. Each cache entry carries a 16-bit generation stamped at insertion. The
neighbour list carries a matching generation per edge. A mismatch on lookup is a hard fault, not a
silent reuse. Cost: 2 bytes per edge and one comparison.
2. Canary edges. A random 0.1% of edges are recomputed every step regardless of the predicate, and
their cached value is compared against the exact one. The running maximum of that discrepancy
is the online error estimate. If it exceeds the declared budget, Frost logs a warning and tightens ε
automatically. This gives continuous in-production validation of the central approximation at 0.1%
cost.

F ROST: I NCREMENTAL GNN I NFERENCE FOR G EOMETRIC ROLLOUTS

60

3. Autograd tape audit. At startup, Frost walks the autograd graph and asserts that every cached tensor
is a leaf with requires_grad=False and that every hot tensor is connected to R. A misplaced
detach (T6) becomes a startup failure rather than a subtly wrong result. Roughly 40 lines and the
highest value-per-line in the codebase.
4. Poisoned slots. Freed cache slots are filled with signalling NaN in debug builds, so reuse of a
tombstoned entry propagates immediately to a visible NaN rather than to a plausible-looking wrong
force.
The canary mechanism deserves emphasis: it means Frost knows its own error at runtime and can be
run in production with a guarantee that is monitored rather than assumed. No prior work in this space —
BoostMD, DMTS, reduced precision — offers an online error estimate.

19.7

T8/T9/T10 — Positioning responses

T8 (workload relevance). Addressed by evaluating on Matbench Discovery at full scale (257,000
relaxations) and on Li3 PO4 and diamond Si, for which published per-atom step times exist [63, 49].
Relevance is demonstrated on the community’s own benchmarks rather than asserted.
T9 (venue fit). Addressed structurally: the paper leads with the caching abstraction, the closuresaturation theorem, and the kernel and memory design; chemistry appears only in the evaluation. The
vocabulary — invalidation, closure, staleness, admission control, generation counters — is deliberately
that of a systems paper.
T10 (concurrent work). Addressed by building on TorchSim and the ALCHEMI kernels rather
than against them, so that upstream progress compounds with rather than threatens Frost; and by
front-loading E1–E4, whose characterisation value is independent of who ships the runtime.

19.8

Additional experiments implied by this section

E15 — Jacobian storage schemes. The admissible schemes of Table 32 — rank-3 FP32, rank-3
BF16, and BF16 with envelope tiering at three thresholds — across four systems. Measure memory,
bandwidth, achieved lifetime extension X against Equation (36), and net speedup. Falsification:
if BF16 storage measurably degrades force error above the tolerance, fall back to FP32 and accept
4×. Additionally, we run the two inadmissible schemes (zeroth-order and radial-only) as a deliberate
demonstration of Theorem 6.1: both should show force error independent of ε and immediate energy
drift. This is the theorem’s empirical confirmation and belongs in the paper.
E16 — Predicate cascade. Ablate the three hierarchy levels independently. Report predicate cost as
a fraction of step time in each configuration. Success: predicate below 3% of step time at φ = 0.1.
E17 — Reweighting validity. Compute Neff /N across systems and tolerances; compare reweighted
equilibrium averages (energy, pressure, RDF) against exact baselines. Success: Neff /N > 0.9 and
reweighted averages within statistical error of exact.
E18 — Canary calibration. Verify that the 0.1% canary estimate tracks the true error measured by
full recomputation on held-out frames. Success: canary estimate within 2× of true maximum error.

F ROST: I NCREMENTAL GNN I NFERENCE FOR G EOMETRIC ROLLOUTS

20

61

Detailed Ablation Analysis

Frost has nine independently switchable mechanisms. A reviewer’s first question after a headline
speedup is which of them earned it, and the second is whether any are decorative. This section specifies
the ablation programme in full: what each component is expected to contribute, on which systems, why,
and what result would justify deleting it from the paper.

20.1

The nine components

20.2

Ablation design

We run three ablation families, each answering a different question.
20.2.1

Family A — Leave-one-out

All components enabled, one disabled. Isolates each component’s marginal contribution in the presence
of the others, which is the honest measure for a system whose parts interact.
20.2.2

Family B — Cumulative build-up

Starting from the baseline, enable components one at a time in dependency order. Isolates each
component’s incremental contribution and produces the figure reviewers actually read: a staircase from
1.0× to the final number, with each step labelled.
20.2.3

Family C — Two-way interactions

Some components are predicted to interact multiplicatively rather than additively. Leave-one-out and
cumulative builds both miss this, so we test the four pairs where we predict interaction.
20.2.4

Family D — Inadmissible configurations, run deliberately

Two configurations are known a priori to be incorrect. We run them anyway, because a theorem that
predicts a catastrophic failure is more convincing when the failure is shown.
The second row is the sharper test. A generic bug produces isotropic error; the theorem predicts error
confined to the transverse directions, which should appear as a selective distortion of bending modes in
the VDOS while stretching modes remain accurate. Observing that signature is strong evidence the
analysis is right.

20.3

Negative ablations

A component that helps everywhere is suspicious. We specify in advance where each component should
not help, and report those cells — a component that helps where the mechanism says it cannot is
evidence of a confound, not of success.

20.4

Sensitivity ablations

Beyond on/off, three components have continuous settings whose sweeps are informative.

20.5

Per-domain ablation coverage

Not every ablation is meaningful in every domain. Table 39 states the coverage matrix so that gaps are
deliberate rather than accidental.

F ROST: I NCREMENTAL GNN I NFERENCE FOR G EOMETRIC ROLLOUTS

20.6

62

Reporting protocol

• Every ablation cell is reported at matched fidelity, not at matched ε. Two configurations with
different ε are not comparable; we tune each to the same measured force error and compare speed
there. This is the standard the diffusion-caching literature converged on, and it is the only fair one.
• Every ablation reports median with IQR over 5 seeds, per §15.2.
• Every cell where the predicted direction is wrong is reported and discussed, not dropped. Family C
exists specifically to surface interactions we did not anticipate.
• Components failing their “delete if” criterion in Table 34 are removed from the system, not merely
omitted from the paper.

20.7

The two rows that decide the paper

Of roughly ninety ablation cells, two carry the argument:
1. Family B, step 2 → 3 (stride → per-edge predicate). This is the spatial-adaptivity claim in a single
number, and by Proposition 19.5 it cannot be negative — only small. If it is small everywhere, the
honest paper is a characterisation of temporal redundancy in geometric rollouts with the finding that
it is spatially uniform, and CV(τ) is the statistic that says so.
2. Family A, C3 disabled. If the first-order cache does not pay for its 4× memory and bandwidth, the
design reverts to zeroth order and the ceiling analysis of §12.3 says the speedup is roughly halved.
This is the single largest quantitative fork in the design.
Both are measurable in the first six weeks, and both are gated in the timeline (Table 44) rather than
discovered at write-up.

21

Why Has This Not Been Done?

Reviewers ask this of any idea that looks simple in hindsight, and a proposal that cannot answer it
invites the suspicion that it has been tried and failed silently. We have four answers, and we think they
are complete.

21.1

Answer 1: the natural version provably fails

The obvious design — cache node features, invalidate on displacement — is not merely inefficient.
Theorem 3.2 shows it is structurally impossible in condensed phases: for MACE-class parameters, a
raw dirty fraction of 1% produces a post-closure dirty fraction above 99% (Corollary 3.3). Anyone who
tries the obvious thing measures no benefit, concludes the redundancy is not exploitable, and stops. The
edge-local reformulation is the non-obvious step, and it is non-obvious precisely because the cost model
points at nodes: there are N of them and 50N edges, so caching edges looks like the more expensive
choice until one works out the closure.

21.2

Answer 2: the two communities do not overlap

Temporal redundancy in MLIP-driven MD has been attacked — by BoostMD, by distilled multiple time
stepping, by TrajCast — entirely from the model side: train a surrogate, distil a cheaper potential, learn
the trajectory update. These are the natural moves for a machine learning or computational chemistry
group.
The systems move — treat the intermediate state as a cache with an invalidation predicate and a
memory manager — requires someone who thinks about caches. Meanwhile the systems community

F ROST: I NCREMENTAL GNN I NFERENCE FOR G EOMETRIC ROLLOUTS

63

working on caching for iterative neural inference has been fully occupied with diffusion transformers,
where the analogous mechanisms (DeepCache, TeaCache, TaylorSeer, SenCache, X-Slim, SoftCap)
have produced a dense literature. Nobody has carried them across.

21.3

Answer 3: the enabling conditions are recent

Three preconditions all arrived within roughly two years:
• Universal foundation MLIPs. Before MACE-MP-0 and its successors, potentials were systemspecific, so a sensitivity calibration Ŝ would have to be redone per system and the engineering would
not amortise. A universal model makes the calibration transferable.
• A published, trusted error figure. Frost’s tolerance argument requires the community to agree
on what a model’s error is. Matbench Discovery and its relatives made that number canonical and
comparable.
• Kernel libraries that isolate the tensor product. cuEquivariance, FlashTP, and OpenEquivariance
created exactly the backend abstraction Frost needs to intercept, which is what keeps the model shim
at roughly one hundred lines instead of a fork.

21.4

Answer 4: the correctness argument is not obvious

The immediate objection to caching in MD is that stale features break energy conservation, and that
objection is correct for the naive implementation. The MD literature has documented the resulting
instabilities since 1993 [7, 43], and the most recent distilled-MTS work still accepts non-conservativity
and compensates architecturally [50]. A practitioner who knows that literature will reject the idea before
implementing it.
The resolution — differentiate through the cached graph, so the forces are the exact gradient
of a well-defined surrogate energy (Proposition 4.3) — is a one-line change in the autograd tape
with a non-obvious consequence, and it is what makes the approach admissible rather than merely
fast. Proposition 19.1 extends it to the first-order cache, where the intuition that a more aggressive
approximation must weaken the guarantee is simply wrong.

21.5

Answer 5: the failure mode is invisible in an energy-only analysis

Theorem 6.1 is the sharpest answer, and we found it only by redoing the analysis in C1 . A practitioner
who reasons about energy error — the natural frame, since MLIPs are energy models and their published
accuracy is an energy or force RMSE — concludes that a zeroth-order cache with a tight tolerance is
accurate. It is not: it deletes the force contribution of every cached edge, with an error that does not
shrink as the tolerance shrinks. Someone attempting the obvious design would observe immediate,
violent energy drift, conclude that caching is fundamentally incompatible with conservative dynamics,
and abandon it — which is precisely the conclusion the multiple-time-stepping literature has already
reached by a different route [7, 50].
The escape is not to cache more carefully. It is to cache the derivative alongside the value, so that
differentiating the cache reproduces the derivative rather than zeroing it. That is a small implementation
change with a large and non-obvious justification, and it is the step we would not have found without
writing the C1 analysis down.

F ROST: I NCREMENTAL GNN I NFERENCE FOR G EOMETRIC ROLLOUTS

64

The summary we will give a reviewer
The obvious version is provably impossible at node granularity; the workable version requires
an idea from a community that does not work on this problem; the enabling infrastructure is
two years old; the correctness argument runs against a well-founded prior that caching breaks
conservation; and the specific failure mode of the naive design is invisible unless the error analysis
is done on gradients rather than values. Any one of these would explain a gap. Together they
explain it comfortably.

22

Energy Efficiency

Frost reduces work, and reduced work is reduced energy. We treat this as a reported axis rather than a
claim, because the measurement is nearly free and the community increasingly asks for it — Matbench
Discovery already reports VRAM and RAM alongside accuracy, and cost-aware model selection is now
routine.

22.1

Why the saving should exceed the speedup

Naively, energy scales with time at fixed power, so a 2× speedup gives a 2× energy saving. Frost
should do slightly better, for two reasons:
• The work removed is the compute-dense work. The tensor product is the arithmetic-intensity peak
of the model; the work that remains (aggregation, predicate) is bandwidth-bound. Removing tensor
products lowers average board power, not just runtime.
• Memory traffic is not increased proportionally. The cache is read once per step for aggregation,
which the baseline also does. Only the first-order path adds reads, and that is the axpy accounted for
in §12.3.
Against this, DRAM access energy is high and the cache enlarges the working set, so the net effect
is genuinely uncertain — which is why we measure rather than assert.

22.2

Metrics and method

Method caveat we will state. NVML board power on consumer and workstation cards samples
coarsely and includes memory and board overheads. We therefore report energy for long runs (minutes, not steps), report idle-corrected figures alongside raw ones, and do not claim per-kernel energy
attribution, which the instrumentation cannot support.
E29 — Energy characterisation. Frost versus baseline on four systems, measuring all five metrics
above, with the DVFS sweep as a secondary axis (since memory-bound phases tolerate lower clocks).
Success: energy saving at least proportional to speedup. Interesting negative: if energy saving is less
than the speedup, the cache’s memory traffic is costing more than the removed arithmetic saves — a
finding worth reporting, and one that would motivate the compressed-Jacobian schemes of §19.1.5.

23

Failure Semantics

What does a user see when Frost is wrong? A system that can fail silently will not be enabled by
default, and a system that is not enabled by default is not adopted. This section specifies the observable
behaviour in each failure mode.

F ROST: I NCREMENTAL GNN I NFERENCE FOR G EOMETRIC ROLLOUTS

65

The design principle
Every failure mode terminates in one of three outcomes: corrected automatically, degraded to
baseline, or loud crash. None terminates in “completes normally with wrong numbers”. That
property, not the speedup, is what determines whether a simulation practitioner will turn Frost on.

23.1

The self-disabling guarantee

Frost’s strongest safety property is that it can turn itself off. If the canary or drift observer indicates the
approximation is not holding, Frost reverts to exact evaluation for the remainder of the trajectory and
records the step at which it did so. The user’s simulation completes, correctly, at baseline speed, with
an audit trail.
We regard this as more valuable than a marginal speedup, and we will say so in the paper. It also
makes the default-on argument tenable: the worst realistic outcome of enabling Frost is that it does
nothing and costs the predicate.

24

Limitations

Distinct from the threats of §18, which are risks that may or may not materialise, these are things Frost
cannot do by construction. We list them because a paper that bounds itself is easier to trust on the claims
it does make.
1. Frost cannot help where geometry is not the state. Eulerian solvers, fixed-mesh weather models,
and any architecture whose graph is static and whose fields evolve independently of node motion
are outside the mechanism entirely. The CylinderFlow control (§13) is included to demonstrate this
rather than to hide it.
2. Frost cannot exceed the aggregation bound. Smax (§12.3) is set by the share of work that is not
edge-local. For architectures with low θ the ceiling is modest regardless of how cold the cache runs.
3. Frost cannot correct dynamical observables by reweighting. Importance reweighting (§19.4)
fixes equilibrium averages. Time-correlation quantities — diffusion coefficients from mean-squared
displacement, vibrational spectra from velocity autocorrelation — depend on the trajectory itself and
are only validated empirically (E14).
√
4. Frost’s reweighting degrades as N. Global energy reweighting has the standard overlap problem
(Appendix E). Frost offers block-local reweighting and tolerance tightening as responses, but the
unbiasedness guarantee is strongest for small and medium systems, which is the opposite of where
the speedup is largest.
5. Frost does not help reactive or molten regimes at scale. By Theorem 15.2, a hot region of Θ(N)
gives no gain. Frost degrades to approximately baseline via admission control rather than becoming
slower, but it does not accelerate.
6. Frost costs memory, and the cost is not optional. The cache is O(EF) for values plus O(3EF) for
the mandatory Jacobian (Theorem 6.1), reducible to roughly 2.5× with a BF16 Jacobian. Table 12
predicts a net memory saving because detached tensors need no retained activations, but that
prediction now requires M ≥ 3 rather than M ≥ 2, and is untested until E10. For M = 2 architectures
the memory claim may not hold and we will report it either way.
7. Frost’s guarantees are weaker outside Hamiltonian dynamics. For GNS and MeshGraphNets
there is no conservation law, hence no drift theorem, no free oracle, and no reweighting (Table 19).

F ROST: I NCREMENTAL GNN I NFERENCE FOR G EOMETRIC ROLLOUTS

66

Roughly one third of the correctness machinery does not transfer, and we did not attempt to disguise
this by inventing a surrogate invariant.
8. Frost is not validated for adaptive remeshing. MeshGraphNets’ sizing-field remesher forces
wholesale invalidation; frequent remeshing would negate the benefit. We evaluate only the nonadaptive setting and say so.
9. Short trajectories may not amortise warm-up. A cold cache costs a full recompute plus Jacobian
storage on the first step for every edge. E28 measures the crossover; below it, Frost is a small net
loss.

25

Broader Implications

Three things the community gains regardless of how Frost’s numbers turn out.

25.1

A negative result that saves other people time

Theorem 3.2 is, we believe, the most durable contribution here. It says that caching node features in
a message-passing GNN over a radius graph is not merely inefficient but structurally impossible in
condensed settings, because the M-hop closure of any non-trivial dirty set is the entire system. The
proof is three lines and contains no physics.
Anyone who has the idea of caching a GNN across timesteps — and it is a natural idea, which is why
several groups have approached it from the model side rather than the systems side — will otherwise
spend weeks discovering this empirically. Publishing it with the constants worked out (Corollary 3.3:
saturation at a 1% raw dirty fraction for MACE-class parameters) closes that path and points at the
edge-local one.

25.2

Tolerance-matched computation as a general principle

The framing — do not compute an approximate model more precisely than its own validated error — is
not specific to interatomic potentials. It applies wherever a learned model has (i) a published accuracy
figure and (ii) an inference path whose cost can be traded against precision. The budget is usually three
to five orders of magnitude, and it is currently spent on nothing.
The mechanism differs by domain — caching here, quantisation elsewhere, early exit elsewhere
again — but the accounting is the same, and it converts an accuracy number from a model card into a
systems resource. We think that reframing is portable and we say so explicitly rather than leaving it
implicit in a chemistry paper.

25.3

A workload class with no systems treatment

Autoregressive geometric rollout — a GNN evaluated 103 –106 times on a slowly deforming graph,
with a mandatory backward pass — is a distinct inference workload with distinct properties, and it
has received essentially no systems attention. It is not LLM serving (no KV cache, no autoregressive
token stream, but a backward pass on every evaluation). It is not diffusion (the iteration is physical time,
not a denoising schedule, and the number of steps is not a design choice). It is not conventional GNN
inference (which assumes a static graph and a single forward pass).
Naming and characterising this class is worth doing independently of Frost, and §16 gives the first
cross-architecture account of what its members have in common.

F ROST: I NCREMENTAL GNN I NFERENCE FOR G EOMETRIC ROLLOUTS

25.4

67

Candidate domains for future work

The last row points at a mechanism we have not developed: MPC evaluates many rollouts from nearby
states, so a cache keyed by geometry rather than by trajectory could be shared across them. That requires
a locality-sensitive key rather than an exact one, and is a research question rather than an extension.

26

Artifact Evaluation

Prepared in the standard form so that the artifact can be evaluated without correspondence.

27

Timeline and Milestones

The plan is twelve weeks with a decision gate at week two and a second at week six.
Gate 1 (week 2). If E1 shows φ > 0.5 at defensible ε on favourable systems, stop. Total sunk cost:
two weeks and one logger. The measurement is publishable as a negative characterisation result in its
own right — “temporal redundancy in MLIP MD is not exploitable at edge granularity” is a useful
finding.
Gate 2 (week 6). If E8 shows fixed-stride caching matching Frost, the spatial adaptivity contribution
collapses. Fallback: pivot to the characterisation paper (E1–E4 plus the closure-saturation theorem),
which is a complete measurement contribution.
Contingency ordering. Note that the schedule front-loads both fatal risks (T1 at week 2, T4 at week
6) and defers the merely difficult ones (attenuated closure, week 8). This is deliberate: the project
should fail early and cheaply if it is going to fail.

28

Revised Risk Position

§19 changed the risk profile materially. This section restates it, because a proposal that lists mitigations
without recomputing the residual risk has not actually managed anything.

28.1

The revised decision structure

The important structural change is that E15a costs nothing extra. The first-order validity radius can be
computed from the same displacement logs E1 already collects, because Ji j is available analytically.
One additional analysis script converts a single-chance gate into a two-chance gate.

28.2

What the paper claims under each outcome

28.3

Revised timeline

29

Conclusion

Frost rests on a single observation: machine-learned interatomic potentials are accurate to tens of
meV/Å and are evaluated to machine precision, a million times per nanosecond, uniformly across atoms
whose local environments have not measurably changed. The gap between model fidelity and arithmetic
precision is a systems resource, and no one has claimed it.
The naive way to claim it fails, and we prove why: node-level caching in message-passing architectures saturates its M-hop closure at any realistic temperature (Theorem 3.2). Frost’s three load-bearing

F ROST: I NCREMENTAL GNN I NFERENCE FOR G EOMETRIC ROLLOUTS

68

contributions are the responses. Caching edge-local geometric quantities rather than node features eliminates closure at the first layer and entirely for strictly local architectures (Proposition 3.4). Propagating
attenuated error bounds rather than binary flags recovers deep layers, using the same over-smoothing
measurements that justify architectural pruning (Theorem 5.2). And differentiating through the cached
graph makes stale evaluation exactly conservative with respect to a surrogate Hamiltonian, converting
energy drift from an empirical hope into a proved bound (Theorem 5.5) — in contrast to distilled
multiple-time-stepping schemes, which knowingly abandon conservativity and must recover stability
architecturally.
Everything else is borrowed and standard, and we name it: Verlet neighbour lists, velocityVerlet integration, pretrained checkpoints, cuEquivariance and FlashTP kernels, TorchSim batching,
LAMMPS integration, CUDA-graph bucketing from LLM serving, the keyframe-reset skeleton and
Taylor-extrapolation idea from diffusion caching [62, 17], sensitivity-driven reuse selection from SenCache [20], and importance reweighting from free-energy methods. That ratio — three or four new
ideas on a bed of well-tested machinery — is what we take a systems contribution to be.
The mechanism is also not chemical, and §13 establishes the scope properly. Theorem 3.2 refers
only to radius graphs and message-passing depth, so it governs any Lagrangian rollout GNN; we
therefore evaluate on GNS and MeshGraphNets alongside interatomic potentials, with an Eulerian CFD
case as a negative control where the mechanism must fail. The transfer is partial and we say so: roughly
two-thirds of Frost carries over, while the conservativity theorem, the drift bound, the free correctness
oracle, and reweighting are all consequences of Hamiltonian dynamics and do not. That asymmetry is
why molecular dynamics stays the flagship rather than one domain among several.
Section 19 then turns the four tractable threats into design. The first-order cache (§19.1) attacks the
fatal redundancy risk directly, extending the validity radius by an order of magnitude or more while
— by Proposition 19.1 — leaving the conservativity guarantee exactly intact, because that guarantee
rests on Ee being smooth in position rather than close to Etot . The hierarchical predicate cascade (§19.2)
reduces invalidation cost toward O(N) and compounds with the first-order cache, since longer validity
means longer deferral. Importance reweighting (§19.4) upgrades the claim from “bounded error” to
“asymptotically unbiased equilibrium averages with a measured effective sample size” — a guarantee no
prior method in this space can offer, because none exposes a surrogate energy whose exact counterpart
is evaluable on the same configuration. And Proposition 19.5 makes fixed-stride caching a point in
Frost’s own configuration space, so the most dangerous comparison cannot be lost, only won by a
margin that CV(τ) predicts in advance.
The proposal is designed to be cheap to refute. The decisive measurement is the joint distribution
of edge-geometry perturbation and induced force error; it requires two weeks, one GPU, and no new
kernels, and it is specified with numerical thresholds fixed in advance. Critically, the same logs answer
the question for both the zeroth- and first-order caches, so the gate has two independent chances to
pass at no additional experimental cost. If both come back unfavourably, the characterisation and the
closure-saturation theorem remain as a contribution — an explanation of why this natural idea has not
been pursued — and the runtime is never built.

30

Extension to Other Atomistic Workloads

Frost is presented for molecular dynamics because MD is where the redundancy argument is strongest
and where the correctness oracle is cleanest. The mechanism, however, applies wherever consecutive
evaluations of an MLIP are related by a small, bounded perturbation of the atomic coordinates. This
section examines four such workloads, states what changes, and identifies which are in scope for the
twelve-week plan and which are follow-on work.

F ROST: I NCREMENTAL GNN I NFERENCE FOR G EOMETRIC ROLLOUTS

30.1

69

Structure relaxation

Geometry optimisation — FIRE, L-BFGS, gradient descent, or the Frechet cell filter variants — iterates
until forces fall below a threshold. It is the dominant workload in high-throughput screening: Matbench
Discovery alone comprises 257,000 relaxations [53, 56].
What is favourable. Relaxation is more amenable to Frost than MD, for a reason that is worth stating
carefully. As a relaxation converges, the step size shrinks monotonically by construction — that is what
convergence means. The per-step geometric perturbation therefore decreases along the trajectory, so the
dirty fraction φ decays as the optimisation proceeds. Frost gets progressively cheaper exactly where
relaxations spend most of their iterations: near the minimum, taking small steps.
What changes. Two things. First, there is no energy-conservation oracle, because relaxation is
dissipative by design. Correctness must instead be assessed on the converged structure: final energy,
final forces, and the resulting stability classification. Second, line searches and momentum-based
optimisers (FIRE) can take large trial steps that invalidate most of the cache; the refresh policy must be
aware of the optimiser’s internal state rather than treating every step as a small perturbation.
Scope. In scope. The TorchSim integration (§10) gives relaxation for free through the existing
autobatcher, and E14 already includes Matbench Discovery at full scale. We expect relaxation to be
Frost’s strongest quantitative result and have deliberately not led with it, because a systems contribution
that works only on the easiest workload is a weaker contribution.

30.2

Nudged elastic band and transition-state search

NEB relaxes a chain of nimg images connecting two minima, with spring forces coupling neighbours.
Each iteration evaluates the MLIP on every image.
The additional opportunity. NEB offers a redundancy axis MD does not: adjacent images are
geometrically similar to one another, not merely to their own past. An edge cache keyed on geometry
rather than on (image, edge) could in principle be shared across images, exploiting spatial-alongthe-path redundancy in addition to temporal redundancy. Whether this is profitable depends on how
similar adjacent images actually are, which varies enormously between a smooth diffusion barrier and a
bond-breaking reaction.
Scope. Out of scope for the twelve-week plan; flagged as the most interesting extension. The crossimage key would require a locality-sensitive hash on edge geometry rather than an exact key, which is a
research question in its own right.

30.3

Phonon and elastic-constant calculations

Finite-difference phonons displace each atom by ±δ (typically 0.01 Å) and evaluate forces, requiring
6N evaluations for a full force constant matrix, or 6Nirr using symmetry.
Why this is nearly ideal. Every displaced configuration differs from the reference by exactly one
atom moved by exactly δ . The dirty set is therefore precisely the edges incident to that atom — typically
n̄ ≈ 50 out of 5 × 105 edges, a dirty fraction of order 10−4 . This is the most extreme cacheability of any
workload we have identified.

F ROST: I NCREMENTAL GNN I NFERENCE FOR G EOMETRIC ROLLOUTS

70

The catch. Finite-difference phonons require force differences to be accurate, and those differences
are small. An approximation that is invisible in absolute forces may be visible in their difference. The
tolerance must therefore be tightened, and the correct comparison is against the finite-difference step
size rather than against the model’s absolute error. We include phonons in E14 specifically because they
are the most demanding fidelity test we can construct, and a favourable result there would be strong
evidence that the approximation is genuinely benign.

30.4

Hybrid Monte Carlo and swap moves

TorchSim supports swap Monte Carlo and hybrid swap MC [56]. A swap exchanges two atoms’ species,
changing the graph’s node attributes but not its geometry.
An interesting asymmetry. Under a species swap, all edge geometries are unchanged — so the purely
geometric factors Yℓm (r̂i j ) and R(ri j ) remain exactly valid. Only the species-dependent components
change, and only on edges incident to the two swapped atoms. A species-aware cache decomposition,
separating the geometric factor from the chemical factor, would make swap moves nearly free. Whether
MLIP implementations factorise cleanly enough to permit this is architecture-dependent and untested.
Scope. Out of scope; recorded as an observation for future work.

30.5

31

Summary of extensions

Deployment, Artefact, and Adoption

A systems paper whose artefact nobody installs has made a weaker contribution than one whose artefact
becomes default. This section states the adoption strategy explicitly, because it shaped several design
decisions that would otherwise look arbitrary.

31.1

The one-line adoption constraint

Frost is designed so that enabling it is a single change at the call site:

Listing 3: Adoption surfaces
Three consequences of this constraint, each of which cost us design freedom:
1. No model modification. Frost intercepts tensor-product call sites through the existing backend
abstraction. This rules out designs that would require changing MACE or NequIP internals, and is
why we cache at the tensor-product boundary rather than at some more favourable internal point.

F ROST: I NCREMENTAL GNN I NFERENCE FOR G EOMETRIC ROLLOUTS

71

2. A single user-facing knob, in physical units. Not a stride, not a cache size, not a hit-rate target — a
force tolerance in meV/Å, defaulted from the checkpoint’s published validation RMSE. A user who
does not know what Frost is should still be able to set this correctly.
3. Safe by default. The admission-control fallback (§8) guarantees Frost is never materially slower
than baseline. A system that can silently make things worse will not be enabled by default, and a
system that is not enabled by default is not adopted.

31.2

Composition with the existing ecosystem

31.3

What we will release

The artefact comprises: the Frost runtime (MIT, matching TorchSim’s licence so that upstreaming is
frictionless); the calibration harness for Ŝ; the differential test suite including the ε = 0 bitwise check;
every simulation cell as a construction script; baseline configurations including the full stride ablation;
and the raw logs behind every figure. We commit to releasing E1–E4 in full regardless of whether the
results support the system, because the characterisation is useful to the community either way.

The estimator is deliberately biased upward: Ŝ must be an upper bound for the predicate to be safe.
Under-estimating Ŝ would let genuinely stale edges pass the test, silently violating the error budget;
over-estimating merely costs efficiency. The maximum, the envelope smoothing, and the safety factor
all push in the safe direction.

Derivation of the First-Order Cache Jacobian

The first-order cache requires Ji j = ∂ gi j /∂ ri j . This appendix shows it is available analytically and is
already computed by the existing backward pass, so that storing it costs memory but essentially no
arithmetic.
Write gi j = R(ri j ) ⊗CG Yℓm (r̂i j ), with ri j = ∥ri j ∥ and r̂i j = ri j /ri j . By the product rule,

Three observations follow.
Both terms already exist. The force computation requires ∂ E/∂ ri j , which by the chain rule contains
exactly ∂ gi j /∂ ri j contracted with the upstream gradient. Frost intercepts the factor before contraction.
In PyTorch this is a hook on the tensor-product module’s backward; in a fused CUDA kernel it is a
second output buffer.
The rank is at most three. Ji j maps R3 into irrep space, so rank(Ji j ) ≤ 3 regardless of nirrep . This
is what makes the low-rank storage scheme of §19.1.5(b) exact rather than approximate up to k = 3;
truncation below k = 3 is where approximation enters.
The projector explains the radial/angular split. The factor (I − r̂r̂⊤ )/ri j is the projector onto
directions perpendicular to the bond, scaled by 1/ri j . It vanishes for pure bond stretching and grows
for bending at short range. Empirically, in stiff solids the radial term dominates because stretching is
the stiffer mode — which is the justification for scheme (a), radial-only storage at 2× memory. E15
measures the ratio directly rather than assuming it.

Writing ∆k = β (Etot − Ee(k) ) and expanding for small ∆,
Neff
≈ 1 − Var(∆) + O(∆3 ),
n

(42)

e
so the estimator degrades only at second order in the energy discrepancy. Since Frost bounds |Etot − E|
by design, Var(∆) is small and Neff /n is near unity. This is the numerical statement of why reweighting
is cheap here and expensive in its usual applications.

F ROST: I NCREMENTAL GNN I NFERENCE FOR G EOMETRIC ROLLOUTS

E.2

76

A worked magnitude

At 300 K, β −1 = kB T ≈ 25.85 meV. Suppose the cached-versus-exact total-energy discrepancy for
a 1,000-atom
system is 1 meV per atom in the worst case and uncorrelated across atoms, giving
√
σ∆E ≈ 1000 × 1 meV ≈ 32 meV and Var(∆) ≈ (32/25.85)2 ≈ 1.5. That is not negligible — Neff /n
would be poor.
This calculation is worth including precisely because it is√
unfavourable. It shows that reweighting
at the level of the total energy of a large system degrades with N, which is the standard difficulty with
global reweighting. Two responses:
1. Tighten ε when reweighting is required. The per-atom discrepancy scales with ε; reducing ε by
10× reduces Var(∆) by 100×. Reweighting is therefore available as a mode, at a cost in speedup,
rather than as a free addition. Frost exposes it as such.
2. Local observables reweight locally. For observables that are sums of local quantities (RDF,
√
coordination number, local stress), block reweighting over spatial subdomains avoids the global N
penalty, because the relevant discrepancy is that of the subdomain, not the whole cell.
We state this limitation explicitly rather than presenting reweighting
as an unqualified fix. E17
√
measures Neff /n across system sizes precisely to establish where the N penalty bites.

78

Detailed Experimental Protocols

This appendix specifies the protocols in enough detail that E1 — the decision gate — can be executed
by someone other than the author without further design work.

H.1

E1: redundancy characterisation

Systems and cells.
Procedure.
1. Equilibrate each system for 20 ps NVT (Langevin, γ = 0.01/fs) at each of 300 K and 1000 K.
Discard.
2. Run 10 000 production steps at ∆t = 1 fs, logging every step.
(e)

3. For each edge e and each step t, record ∥ri j (t) − ri j (t0 )∥ under a simulated cache with per-edge
reference times, for each of eight tolerance values spanning ε ∈ [0.1, 50] meV/Å.
4. Independently, for 100 randomly chosen frames, recompute forces exactly and with the simulated
stale cache; record the induced force error per atom.
The decisive plot. A two-dimensional histogram with ∥∆ri j ∥ on the abscissa and induced per-atom
force error on the ordinate, with the model’s validated force RMSE drawn as a horizontal line and the
resulting φ annotated. If the mass of the distribution sits well below that line at a φ satisfying Table 5,
the project proceeds.
Instrumentation cost. No kernels. A PyTorch forward hook on the tensor-product module, a NumPy
logger, and post-processing. Estimated 300 lines and two days to write; the remainder of the two weeks
is compute and analysis.

H.2

E3: attenuation measurement

For each layer t and each of 50 sampled configurations:
1. Perturb a single cached edge tensor gi j by a controlled η in a random direction in irrep space.
(t+1)

2. Propagate forward and record ∥∆hi

∥/η for the receiving node.

3. Take the maximum over edges and configurations to obtain αt as a supremum, not a mean — the
bound in Theorem 5.2 requires it.
Cross-validate against linear CKA between successive layer representations, following the protocol
of [63], to confirm that our αt tracks their independently measured redundancy signal. Agreement
between two unrelated measurements of the same phenomenon is worth a paragraph in the paper.

H.3

E7: drift study

Four systems (Si, silica glass, liquid water, Li3 PO4 ) × three mechanisms (none, envelope switching,
velocity rescaling) × four tolerances × three seeds = 144 runs of 100 ps NVE each. At ∆t = 1 fs this is
105 steps per run.
Analysis. Fit D by linear regression on Etot + K over the final 80 ps, discarding the first 20 ps as
transient. Report D against both K (number of√refreshes) and ε to test the two scalings predicted by
Theorem 5.5: linear in Kδmax in the worst case, K under sign independence. Which of the two scalings
is observed is itself a result, since it reveals whether refresh events are correlated.

F ROST: I NCREMENTAL GNN I NFERENCE FOR G EOMETRIC ROLLOUTS

H.4

79

E8: stride ablation

For each system and model, sweep fixed stride k ∈ {2, 5, 10, 25, 50, 100} and Frost tolerance ε over
eight values. Plot both as Pareto fronts in (force error, speedup) space.
Reporting commitment. We will present this figure whatever it shows. If the two fronts coincide,
the paper’s claim is falsified and we say so. If Frost dominates, the margin is the contribution. We
additionally report the spatial statistic that would explain a Frost win: the coefficient of variation of
per-edge refresh interval. A fixed stride is optimal precisely when that coefficient is zero.

I

Failure-Mode Catalogue

Recorded during design, for the benefit of whoever debugs this.

J

Extended Comparison Matrix

The column that matters is “spatially adaptive”. Every prior method treats all atoms identically and
varies only when work is done, not where. Frost’s entire claim is that where matters, and E8 is the
experiment that tests it.

M

Glossary for Systems Readers

N

Sensitivity of the Speedup Model to Its Parameters

The predictions in Tables 5 and 47 rest on parameters that E1–E3 will measure. This appendix shows
how the conclusion moves as those parameters move, so that a reader can judge which measurements
actually matter.

N.1

One-at-a-time sensitivity

The conclusion is unambiguous and useful for planning: only φ and θ matter. Every overhead we
have introduced — predicate, cascade bookkeeping, Taylor evaluation, canary validation — is second
order. This justifies spending engineering effort on lowering φ (the first-order cache) rather than on
shaving overheads, and it justifies the two-week gate being about φ alone.

N.2

Break-even contours

Setting S = 1.3 (the threshold below which we judge the work not worth publishing) and solving for
φ:
Read this as the tolerance the project has for an unfavourable E1. On Allegro (θ ≈ 0.85) Frost
survives a dirty fraction as high as 0.73; on MACE layer-1-only (θ ≈ 0.45) it needs φ below roughly
0.3. This is the quantitative reason the evaluation leads with Allegro, and it is worth stating in the paper
rather than letting it look like an arbitrary choice.

O

Relationship to Classical Molecular Dynamics Techniques

Several Frost mechanisms are recognisable to a classical MD practitioner. Making the correspondence
explicit both credits the prior art and clarifies what is genuinely new.
We regard this table as strengthening rather than weakening the proposal. A design whose components have all been battle-tested in another context is more likely to work than one assembled from
novel parts, and the three or four ideas that remain genuinely new are easier to evaluate when they are
not surrounded by incidental novelty.

F ROST: I NCREMENTAL GNN I NFERENCE FOR G EOMETRIC ROLLOUTS

P

83

Anticipated Reviewer Questions

“Isn’t this just multiple time stepping?” No. MTS splits by force component on a fixed stride, treating every atom identically. Frost splits by edge, adaptively, driven by local geometry. Proposition 19.5
makes fixed-stride caching a single point in Frost’s configuration space; the contribution is the rest of
the space, and CV(τ) quantifies how much of it matters.
“Isn’t this just BoostMD?” No. BoostMD trains a surrogate that consumes cached embeddings [8].
Frost trains nothing, works with any pretrained checkpoint, adapts per edge, offers an error bound, and
preserves conservativity. The two compose.
“Why not just use a cheaper model?” Because that changes the physics. Frost’s approximation is
bounded by the model’s own validated error and is monitored online by the canary; a smaller model’s
error is unbounded relative to the larger one and is not monitored at all. They are also composable:
Frost applies to the distilled student unchanged.
“How
√ do you know the ensemble is unbiased?” Equation (38) with Neff reported per run, subject to
the N caveat quantified in Appendix E. For dynamical observables we rely on direct comparison in
E14 and say so.
“Does this work on GPUs I care about?” E13 repeats the core experiments on A100 and H100. φ is
a physical quantity and is hardware-independent; θ shifts with kernel efficiency, which is exactly why
the experiment exists.
“What if cuEquivariance gets faster?” Then θ falls and Frost’s ceiling falls with it — and Frost still
helps, because it composes multiplicatively. The two optimise orthogonal quantities: cost per tensor
product versus number of tensor products.
“Why not just use a smaller cutoff radius?” Because that changes the model. Edge count scales as
rc3 , so shrinking rc does reduce work — but it silently alters the potential the network was trained to
represent, with an error that is unbounded, uncharacterised, and not monitored. Frost’s error is bounded
by construction, tied to the model’s own validated accuracy, and measured online by the canary. The
two are also composable: Frost applies unchanged to a model retrained at a smaller cutoff.
“Your predicate is just a heuristic threshold.” Theorem 12.1 shows it is the KKT-optimal policy
for the constrained allocation problem under a separable error model, using the same Lagrangian
device RDKV applies to KV cache bit allocation [25]. E25 measures the gap against an exact dynamicprogramming oracle. The assumption that could break it — error correlation — is named, and measured
in E6.
“Energy drift is not a valid check for thermostatted runs.” Correct, and §12.4 addresses it:
extended-Hamiltonian drift under Nosé–Hoover and MTK, periodic NVE probe segments under stochastic thermostats, and the ensemble-independent canary residual as the universal fallback. E24 validates
that each oracle actually detects an injected fault.
“Variable dirty fraction makes latency unpredictable.” True, and it is a known issue for runtimetriggered caching generally [23]. For throughput-oriented MD it is irrelevant. For latency-sensitive use,
§12.5 provides a bounded mode that admits edges in the optimal ηe /ce order and defers the remainder
with the residual carried forward. We report p99 everywhere.

F ROST: I NCREMENTAL GNN I NFERENCE FOR G EOMETRIC ROLLOUTS

84

“How do I choose the tolerance?” You do not. §12.2 exposes an accuracy target instead, and a PI
loop in log ε finds the tolerance that achieves it, using a measured rather than an estimated error signal
— which is what distinguishes it from budget-controlled caching [26].
“Why should a systems venue care about chemistry?” The contribution is a caching abstraction
with a proved invalidation semantics, an O(1)-amortised hierarchical predicate, a memory-hierarchyaware layout, and a correctness argument grounded in a conservation law. The workload happens to be
atomistic simulation; the ideas are about incremental computation on dynamic graphs with a mandatory
backward pass, which is a workload class with no existing systems treatment.

Q

Worked example: dirty fraction on a model system

To make the go/no-go criterion concrete, consider crystalline silicon at 300 K, rc = 5 Å, n̄ ≈ 30, MACEmedium.
Take a target force tolerance ε = 2 meV/Å, an order√of magnitude below a typical fine-tuned
model error. Under the independent budget, εedge = 2/ 30 ≈ 0.37 meV/Å. By Corollary 6.3 the
relevant sensitivity is curvature-based, Ŝ = LE ∥∂ 2 g/∂ r2i j ∥, not slope-based. Suppose calibration returns
Ŝ ≈ 60 meV Å−3 at mid-range distances — smaller than the corresponding slope figure by roughly
the envelope decay length, since H ∼ J/ℓ with ℓ ≈ 3 Å for the smooth cutoffs MLIPs use. Then the
geometric threshold is
εedge
0.37
∥∆ri j ∥max =
≈
≈ 6.2 × 10−3 Å.
(44)
60
Ŝ
The correction that cost memory also bought reach
The slope-based estimate this appendix originally carried was 1.8 × 10−3 Å. Mandating the
Jacobian (Theorem 6.1) raised storage from 1× to 2.5× — but because the first-order cache
is accurate to the curvature rather than the slope, it simultaneously widened the admissible
displacement by roughly 3.4×. The two effects substantially cancel in net speedup. The C1
correction is therefore not simply a cost.
Comparing against the per-step edge perturbation estimated in §3.1 (∼ 1 × 10−3 Å for a stiff solid,
and less for correlated modes), a typical edge now survives several steps before invalidation — giving φ
in the range 0.1–0.3 and, from Table 47 at θ ≈ 0.5, a speedup of roughly 1.5–1.8× for MACE with
layer-1 caching.
Two things follow. First, the estimate remains marginal for MACE at the tightest tolerance — which
is exactly why the evaluation leads with Allegro, where θ ≈ 0.85 and the same φ yields 2.2–3.4×.
Second, the estimate is highly sensitive to Ŝ and to the correlation exponent γ, neither of which we can
predict reliably from first principles. This is the honest reason E1 and E6 must run before anything is
built, and why we have specified numerical gates rather than expressing confidence.

Figure 10: Closed-loop accuracy control. Unlike budget-controlled caching [26], the feedback signal is
measured rather than estimated: the canary residual is an exact sample of the cache error, and drift is a
physical invariant. The user sets an accuracy target; the system finds the tolerance that achieves it.

Table 26: Statistical protocol, fixed before data collection.
Issue

Protocol

Repetitions

Minimum 5 seeds per configuration for stochastic quantities (rollouts, thermostatted
MD); 3 for deterministic timing.
Median with interquartile range, not mean with standard deviation. MLIP and GNN
inference in FP32 is not bitwise deterministic across batch composition, so timing
distributions are right-skewed by scheduler noise.
Discard first 100 steps (warm-up, CUDA-graph capture, cache fill). Report steady-state
only, and report the warm-up cost separately — it is a real cost and hiding it would be
dishonest.
Every baseline runs the same kernels, precision, and neighbour-list settings as Frost.
Speedups against an unoptimised baseline are reported separately and labelled as such.
Learned-simulator rollouts diverge chaotically. We report the divergence step
distribution over seeds rather than a single trajectory’s error, and use paired comparisons
(same initial condition, same noise seed) throughout.
The 26 experiments test distinct hypotheses, not one hypothesis 26 ways. Where a
family of related tests is run (e.g. 8 tolerances × 5 systems in E1), we apply
Holm–Bonferroni within the family and say so.
Falsification thresholds for every experiment are fixed in Appendix L before data
collection. Any post-hoc change is reported as such.
E1–E4, E19, and the CylinderFlow control are reported in full regardless of outcome.

Figure 11: Threat–mitigation map. Red: threats from §18. Green: the design responses developed in
this section. The two load-bearing additions are the first-order cache (which attacks the fatal redundancy
risk directly rather than hoping the measurement is favourable) and importance reweighting (which
converts a bounded force error into an unbiased ensemble estimate).

Figure 12: Hierarchical predicate cascade. Each level is a provably conservative filter: anything it
declares clean is clean, so correctness never depends on the hierarchy, only efficiency. Cost falls from
O(E) per step toward O(N + Ecand ), and in a quiescent system the global level can skip whole steps.

§12.2

The base mechanism, necessarily first-order (Thm. 6.1).
Effect scales with θ and 1 − φ . Without it there is no system.
The spatial-adaptivity claim. Effect scales with CV(τ), the
coefficient of variation of refresh interval. Zero effect if
CV = 0.
Not whether to store J (mandatory) but at what precision and
with what envelope tiering. Trades memory and bandwidth
against residual force error.
Reduces predicate cost toward O(N). Effect largest at low φ ,
where the predicate would otherwise dominate.
Skips tests entirely. Compounds with C3: longer validity ⇒
longer deferral.
Extends caching past layer 1. Zero effect on Allegro (already
zero closure); largest on NequIP (M=3–6).
Drift only. No speed effect. Tested by the diatomic
smoothness metrics (§14).
Correctness only. Costs 1/m of a full evaluation per retained
frame.
Usability and robustness. May cost speed relative to a
hand-tuned ε; the claim is that it removes the tuning, not that
it beats it.

The first-order cache extends the validity radius, which directly lengthens the

Lower φ means the predicate is a larger share of what remains, so the cascade

Item

Detail

Artifact

Source code, calibration harness, test suite, all simulation cells as scripts, baseline
configurations, raw logs, plotting scripts. MIT licence.
1× GPU with ≥24 GB (compute capability ≥7.0) for the core results; 2× for the
multi-GPU experiments. Full reproduction on 2×RTX A6000.
CUDA 12.x, PyTorch 2.x, ASE, TorchSim, LAMMPS (optional, for the large-system path),
DeepMind GNS/MGN reference implementations. Pinned in a lockfile and a container.
All public: MPtrj, OMat24, MatPES, Matbench Discovery, SPICE2, MD17/rMD17,
MD22, DeepMind GNS and MeshGraphNets datasets. Download scripts provided; no
proprietary data.
All public pretrained checkpoints, revisions pinned. No model is trained by us.
Kick-the-tyres: 30 min (E1 on one small system). Core claims: ∼20 GPU-hours. Full suite
including Matbench Discovery: ∼400 GPU-hours. Tiered scripts provided for each level.
Exact reproduction is not possible in FP32 (batch-composition non-determinism,
pre-existing). The ε=0 differential test is the bitwise check; all other results reproduce
within reported IQR.
Every numbered claim in the paper maps to a script and a log file, listed in CLAIMS.md.

Outcome

Claim

Best case

First edge-local cache for MLIP inference; conservative by construction;
unbiased via reweighting; 2–4× on Allegro, 1.5–2× on MACE, larger systems
in the same memory. Full systems paper.
As above but restricted to strictly local architectures and layer 1;
closure-saturation theorem reported as the reason. Still a systems paper,
narrower.
Characterisation paper: temporal redundancy in MLIP MD is real and
exploitable, but spatially uniform; CV(τ) is the statistic that predicts when
adaptivity pays, and it is small in condensed phases. Useful negative result.
Systems paper scoped to MLIP MD, with the negative cross-domain result
reported as a finding: geometric redundancy is present in atomistic dynamics
but not in learned simulators trained with aggressive noise augmentation.
Narrows the claim, does not invalidate it.
Characterisation paper: the closure-saturation theorem plus measurements
showing MLIP features are more sensitive to atomic motion than the
smoothness of the potential suggests. Explains why nobody has done this and
closes the question.

Attenuation fails

Stride matches Frost

Cross-domain fails, MD works

No redundancy at any order

107

Term

Meaning, in systems terms

DFT

Density functional theory. The expensive ground-truth solver MLIPs replace;
roughly O(N 3 ) and limited to hundreds of atoms.
Machine-learned interatomic potential / force field. The learned surrogate.
Radius beyond which interactions are truncated. Defines the graph.
The edge list, maintained incrementally with a skin buffer.
Slack in the neighbour radius so the list survives several steps. Directly
analogous to Frost’s tolerance, one level down.
Ensembles: constant energy / temperature / pressure. NVE is the one with a
conservation law, hence our correctness oracle.
Typically 0.5–2 fs; bounded by the fastest vibration.
Simulated nanoseconds per wall-clock day. The field’s throughput metric.
The network’s output transforms correctly under rotation. Buys data
efficiency; costs the tensor product.
Irreducible representations of SO(3); the “channels” of an equivariant feature.
The equivariant analogue of a matmul. The FLOP bottleneck.
Radial distribution function / vibrational density of states. Structural and
dynamical observables.
Second derivatives of the energy. The most force-error-sensitive observable.
Loop that detects out-of-distribution configurations during MD and calls DFT
on them.
Python simulation toolkit / production C++ MD engine. Frost integrates with
both.
